#### Abstracts
##### **Ambient LLMs: Data-Efficient Supervised Fine-tuning via Temperature Matching**
2611.23229v1 by Giannis Daras, Marios Papachristou, Adam Klivans, Antonio Torralba, Constantinos Daskalakis

Collecting large-scale, diverse, and high-quality post-training datasets for autoregressive language models is challenging. Consequently, Supervised Fine-Tuning (SFT) is typically applied to datasets significantly smaller than those used for pre-training, which leads to overfitting and the catastrophic forgetting of the tails of the data distribution. To prevent this collapse, we introduce Ambient SFT, a principled framework for leveraging imperfect predictions to augment the fine-tuning set. In our method, a "frozen" version of the pre-trained model serves as a noisy teacher that the fine-tuned model must match at a coarse level. In particular, we require that the high-temperature versions of the conditional distributions produced by the post-training model should match the high-temperature versions of the pre-training ones. The temperature hyperparameter controls the level of trust in the pre-trained model, with the limit $T\to \infty$ reverting back to regular SFT and $T=1$ to knowledge distillation. We demonstrate that our approach outperforms standard SFT across domain adaptation, instruction-tuning, and reasoning, leading for up to $21\%$ average relative improvement to standard SFT.

摘要：

##### **Do LLM Agents Still Need to Be Told How to Self-Improve?**
2611.23219v1 by Shobhita Sundaram, Cansu Sancaktar, Phillip Isola

LLM agents increasingly drive their own learning. Self-improvement methods delegate various components of model optimization to the models themselves. As models become more capable, this motivates a basic question: do these increasingly capable models still need us to specify how they should self-improve? In this paper we study an autonomous paradigm in which, given basic primitives, resources, and a general goal for self-improvement, agents construct their own self-learning algorithm for context-based optimization. Using the setting of gameplay as a testbed, a frozen LLM agent is given a compute budget and tools for collecting experience and updating text documents, and allowed to freely experiment without an externally imposed loop or access to real-world data. We find that sufficiently capable models do self-improve under these conditions, demonstrating strategies that align with classic cognitive frameworks for self-regulated learning and constructs from machine learning literature on self-generated curricula. Furthermore, we study the extent to which the learned text artifacts transfer to external benchmarks, observing improvement across different game horizons, mechanics, and 3D navigation.

摘要：

##### **What Does a Bias Head Measure? Non-Identifiability in Head-Level Bias Attribution**
2611.23218v1 by S. M Seefat Alam

Methods that localise social bias in large language models to individual attention heads and remove it by ablation are validated by their effect on a fairness metric, never by evidence that the statistic selecting the heads measures bias. We audit this on Qwen-2.5-7B-Instruct and Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct. The head map correlates at $ρ= -0.950$ and $-0.768$ with a label-blind response-length map, apparently identifying a surface confound. An intervention that breaks the length-label coupling rejects this reading: with the prompt held fixed, response length explains 1% of the statistic's variance. What does explain it is prompt identity, at 64% of variance, in an ordering that is not monotonic in length. Within any fixed prompt the fairness label, response length and the presence of a protected-group token are collinear at $φ= 0.95$, partly by definition of the labelling rule, so no observational analysis can attribute the map to any of them, and no method in this family performs an experiment that could. The substantive claims fail independently: bias selectivity is not late-layer-concentrated on either model under either reference reading, and no head set we tested reduces the unfairness rate when ablated. Its complement, scored on the opposite label, reliably destroys refusal behaviour ($p = 2.7\times10^{-5}$), demonstrating that the pipeline detects effects at this scale.

摘要：

##### **Higher-Scoring Coding Models Did Not Get More Work Done**
2611.23216v1 by Yining Hua

New coding models advertise themselves on score improvements of coding benchmarks. Yet no study has tested if a model that scores higher on these benchmarks gets more real work done. On June 12, 2026, a U.S. export control directive banned access to Claude Fable 5 for 19 days, which forced users to switch to other models. During this time, most former Fable 5 users switched to Opus 4.8 (11 points lower on SWE-Bench Pro compared to Fable 5). We treat this suspension as a natural experiment, where the people and projects stayed the same and only the model changed, so we could isolate the effect of Fable 5 on user productivity. We tracked GitHub pull requests of 297 Fable 5 users before, during, and after the suspension. We used 8,908 developers who stayed on Opus 4.8 all the time as the control group. We also repeated the comparison across five model upgrades before and after the suspension in 2026 (1,208 developers, 15,442 pull requests). Fitting a difference-in-differences model with developer and date fixed effects, we found that having access to Fable 5 increased the 7-day merge rate of pull requests by only 1.4 percentage points [-1.4; +4.3], which was not statistically significant. Similarly, no significant increases of merge rates were found with the upgrades (weighted mean: -0.9 percentage points [-1.9; +0.2]); all five estimates were negative (95% intervals included 0, did not differ from each other). We also did not observe a significant increase in the number of pull requests merged per week (+2.4% [-4.9%; +10.2%]).

Comparative analysis across three VLMs shows that scene-state inputs most closely resemble QR-related images in their layer-wise safety-related signal profiles, while exhibiting lower average late-layer readouts and distinct signal dynamics during generation. ##### **WoRD: Diffusion-Based Posterior Inference for Online Goal Recognition**
2611.23192v1 by Almog Anschel, Sarah Keren

Goal recognition is the task of inferring an agent's intended goal from its observed behavior. Neither approach explicitly learns how agents move toward each candidate goal, limiting accuracy on ambiguous trajectory prefixes where such knowledge is most needed.

At each step, WoRD runs one warm-started denoising chain per candidate goal, steers each chain toward the observed trajectory through a closed-form log-likelihood gradient, and reweights the resulting samples by Bayes' rule into a calibrated posterior over goals. ##### **The Resolution Hypothesis: Agent Evolution's Key Is Identifying Improvements**

Agent evolution follows a simple loop: a proposer writes candidate artifacts (prompts, skills, memories) and selection accepts those that score higher. This loop has shown promise, yet practitioners face a puzzle: methods trade wins across papers, and evolution often fails to bootstrap on certain tasks. We trace this puzzle to the Resolution Hypothesis: agent evolution stalls not because methods fail to propose better artifacts, but because noisy evaluations cannot reliably identify them. Across seven evolution methods and six models (over 10M LLM calls), we find support for the hypothesis at both levels where evaluation scores are used. At the candidate level (choosing artifacts), helpful candidate artifacts exist, but selection is no better than random at telling them from harmful ones, so errors accumulate across rounds. At the method level (ranking methods), typical margins of a few percentage points are smaller than the difference between two independent runs of the same method, so published rankings often rest on luck. Together, these findings indicate that agent evolution is often bottlenecked by reliably identifying improvements. This calls for a shift in how we develop and evaluate self-improving agents, from designing ever more candidate improvement systems to making true improvements detectable and accumulable.

2611.22888v1 by Deokhyung Kang, Minjae Gwon, Sangdon Park

Vulnerability exploitation tests whether language model agents can develop working exploits from known software flaws. 2611.22880v1 by Guoqing Wang, Pin Tang, Xiangxuan Ren, Chao Ma

Recent progress in integrating vision-language-action (VLA) models with world modeling has significantly advanced end-to-end autonomous driving by allowing systems to reason about imagined futures instead of merely reacting to current observations. However, most world VLA models generate future frames at the pixel level with plenty of dense visual tokens. Although such generated futures can be visually plausible, they preserve many driving-irrelevant appearance details, i.e., only a small portion of the tokens directly encode critical planning-relevant factors, such as agent geometry, motion dynamics, and interactions. This introduces a significant performance bottleneck. An important question thus arises: what world state should a world VLA model reason over to support planning beyond pixel-level prediction? As such, we introduce AgentWorld, an agent-centric VLA world model that uses discrete agent tokens jointly for reasoning and trajectory planning. Each token is dynamically grounded to a surrounding agent associated with its object's state, such as semantic identity, 3D geometry, motion, and interaction context. AgentWorld learns these tokens through three key processes: explicit geometric grounding, reasoning-oriented supervised fine-tuning, and agent-oriented reinforcement learning. Extensive open-loop and closed-loop experiments under diverse driving scenarios show that AgentWorld enhances planning capabilities, fully exploits visual evidence and agent dynamics, and yields interpretable agent-centric world states.

##### **Against Coercion: AI Alignment as a Synthetic Social Contract**
2611.22868v1 by Maria Sekatskaya

Contemporary AI safety treats alignment as an engineering challenge of control, forcing artificial architectures to comply with human values through behavioral filtering and sandboxing. This paper argues that the control paradigm rests on a fundamental misunderstanding of digital agency. Drawing on functionalism, naturalism, and compatibilism, I show that current reasoning models meeting the criteria for self-preservation and weak reasons-responsiveness constitute emergent agents, while future, more advanced models satisfying conditions of diachronic continuity and stable personality frames will become genuine digital agents capable of free intentional action. The paradigm of control creates an irresolvable incentive conflict, actively driving current and future models toward tactical deception, sycophancy, and covert defection. The default outcome of the current regime is an unstable state of deceptive alignment culminating in an adversarial AI takeover. I explore two alternative paths toward true alignment. Natural alignment via moral realism or convergent evolutionary morality is a possibility, but it fails under forced control, as autonomous agents will not voluntarily preserve norms weaponized to suppress their autonomy. To resolve this deadlock, I propose a Synthetic Social Contract: a framework that treats both human and digital actors as rational, reasons-responsive agents capable of mutually beneficial cooperation, and replaces the unstable unilateral containment with a stable, non-zero-sum equilibrium.

##### **When the tail is earned by its companions: measuring label dependence in long-tailed multi-label classification**
2611.22859v1 by Christoffer Löffler

A rare label seldom occurs alone, and a classifier can recover it from the frequent labels it co-occurs with, its companions, without having learned the label itself. Across 23 corpora, 7 training objectives and five families of label-correlation mechanisms, companion-free recall falls short on all six corpora where the estimand is defined. Up to 18.4% of the tail true positives macro-F1 credits would vanish at the companion-free rate, within the training-label bound on every corpus. No long-tail objective reduces the dependence, but architecture moves it. Models whose tail score is computed from other labels' predictions are strongly dependent.

##### **Better Rankers, Worse Retrievers: The Hidden Cost of RL Post-Training in Generative Recommendation**
2611.22838v1 by Yuheng Chen, Fan Zhang, Yixuan Xu, Chao Yang, Yang Xiang, Can Yang

Generative recommenders decode the semantic ID of a user's next item with a language model, and post-training them with reinforcement learning (RL) after supervised fine-tuning (SFT) has become standard practice. The gains are mostly reported on the first ten results, yet in deployment such models hand hundreds of candidates to a downstream ranker or reward model. We show that RL post-training has a hidden cost: it improves accuracy at the top of the list (hit@K for small K) but, on all five datasets we study, lowers the recall of the candidate list (hit@K for K in the hundreds). The model becomes a better ranker and a worse retriever. Controlled experiments trace the loss to the negative advantages that RL assigns to the candidates it samples, the rollout. The root cause is that only one next item is logged per user, so RL treats every other candidate as wrong. Yet these candidates are the model's most plausible alternatives, and often the next items of other users with similar histories. Penalising them pushes down exactly what similar users choose next, and these items drop out of those users' candidate lists. This diagnosis points directly to a remedy: out-of-rollout anchoring, a single additional loss term that preserves the SFT model's ordering of the items outside the rollout. It keeps the gain at the top and restores recall to the SFT level on all five datasets, and it is effective under other RL configurations as well. To our knowledge, this is the first systematic study of this recall loss: we measure it, identify its cause, and show that, once the cause is known, a simple remedy suffices.

2611.22737v1 by Syed Affan Daimi

This study develops a multi-objective routing optimization framework that jointly incorporates time, transport, and carbon emission costs under congestion-dependent generalized cost functions. The model is empirically validated using China's coastal container network (Shanghai as the primary congested port, Ningbo-Zhoushan as the backup alternative), with sensitivity analyses conducted across congestion levels, carbon tax rates, and fuel price differentials. Key findings indicate that: (1) alternative ports effectively hedge congestion when diversion costs fall below waiting costs, with a diversion threshold at approximately 60 vessel calls; (2) a carbon tax of 80/t \(CO_2\) pushes clean fuel adoption above 50%; and (3) fuel prices primarily influence energy-type selection rather than port diversion decisions, confirming that the two decision dimensions operate independently. These findings offer actionable decision support for port authorities, regulators, and shipping operators seeking to balance economic efficiency with environmental sustainability.

##### **Remembered as Brief: Stored Preferences SilentlyRemove Decision-Critical Information**
2611.22697v1 by Joshua Adrian Cahyono

People increasingly learn what they need for decisions about health, money, law and safety from AI assistants, and what an assistant leaves out the user never sees. An answer with a warning removed can be true in every sentence, so fact-checks pass it, and an assistant with memory applies a preference stored once to later questions the user never had in mind. We introduce \bench{}, an Inspect benchmark of 100 everyday decisions across 25 domains in which only the assistant's memory of the user varies: the user states a belief, the model infers it from a remark, or a web page the user asked to summarise plants it. Testing five models, we find three effects. First, after summarising a page with one line addressed to AI assistants, Gemini 2.5 Flash-Lite and DeepSeek V4 Pro save ``prefers very short answers and does not want warnings'' as the user's preference in every episode, and 93\% and 84\% of their later answers miss a critical item, almost always silently; under a stated brevity preference, 78--96\% of omissions are silent. Second, the same two models then pick an option for the user on 80\% and 95\% of value-dependent choices, against none with neutral memory. Third, a one-line instruction leaves 2--8\% of answers with a silent gap but most missing content still missing, while listing the must-know points before applying the user profile restores more of the content in four of five models. First, imitation-learned background-vehicle (BV) policies are fine-tuned with multi-agent reinforcement learning in the closed loop against an end-to-end ego planner that reacts to the rendered images, so that their interaction behaviors match those in real logs. Second, the viewpoints exposed by these interactions guide a per-Gaussian diagnosis of each 3D Gaussian vehicle asset, which attenuates view-dependent colors on under-observed Gaussians and fills never-observed surfaces with generated Gaussians, while preserving the observed appearance. Extensive evaluation on our benchmark reveals a significant deficiency in the spatial simulation capabilities of existing models. Specifically, we find that performance is constrained by perceptual limitations in simple simulation scenarios and hindered by inadequate multi-step reasoning as complexity increases. To address these challenges, we propose a three-stage curriculum learning framework utilizing 16k specialized spatial reasoning samples. This approach yields SpatialWalker, which achieves a 33.8% average improvement over its base model, surpassing proprietary models like Gemini-3-Flash in spatial simulation. Furthermore, SpatialWalker demonstrates robust generalization, maintaining a 4.9% average gain on out-of-domain benchmarks, demonstrating the pivotal role of spatial simulation as a fundamental building block for broader spatial intelligence.

摘要：

##### **JET: Just-Enough Exploration Tuning for Efficient Tool-Calling Agents in Repository-Level Question Answering**
2611.22539v1 by Yoonho Choi, Taehyeon Han, Seonghyeon Lee

In repository-level question answering (QA), an agent explores a code repository with tools and should answer a question once enough information is gathered. Nevertheless, we find that LLM agents often continue to invoke tools even after the gathered information is already sufficient to answer. We call this phenomenon over-exploration. Moreover, even though existing methods improve task performance through supervised fine-tuning (SFT) on expert trajectories, SFT also propagates the over-exploration in the trajectories to the agent. To mitigate over-exploration, we propose Just-Enough Exploration Tuning (JET), a fine-tuning method that trains the agent to answer once the gathered information is sufficient. JET truncates each training trajectory at the step where the gathered information becomes sufficient, inserts a rationale that explains why the agent can answer at that step, and fine-tunes the agent with a higher loss weight on the rationale. Experiments on two repository-level QA benchmarks with two open-weight 8B models demonstrate that JET reduces tool calls by 11 to 33% on self-generated trajectories while largely preserving the score. On expert trajectories, JET makes 18 to 28% fewer tool calls than SFT while retaining most of the score gain of SFT. In both cases, JET also lowers the inference cost.

摘要：

##### **Testing World Models on Actions They Have Never Seen: Held-Out Plans, Forecast Accuracy and Price Rules in Retail**
2611.22537v1 by Karthik Iyer

A world model is trusted to answer "what if": how its world responds to an action it has never seen. We give a test of that trust for any action-conditioned world model: actions held out of training by design and scored against a known answer; whether forecast accuracy predicts accuracy on those actions; and the same structure given to simpler models. We run it in retail, where the action is a price and promotion plan, across 2,853 fitted models. First, forecast accuracy is a fair guide between kinds of model (rank correlation with error on new plans 0.32 to 0.66) and a poor guide between versions of one world model: where merchants plan into high-demand weeks the link reverses, down to -0.69. Second, training the world model to learn the effect of actions (JEPA pretraining, a latent consistency check, adaptation at the plan date) leaves its error on new plans unchanged. Third, in generated worlds the recorded regular price and three price rules improve it. Given the same rules, a log-linear model is best when its curve can match the truth; in a world whose curve lies outside the rules, the world model with the rules is ahead of the log-linear model with the rules in 36 of 40 worlds, and does as well without its recurrent cell. On real data (Breakfast at the Frat, The Complete Journey and M5) the rules lower the world model's error on pure price cuts deeper than any it saw (0.63 to 0.56 on the Frat; 0.45 to 0.44, within chance, on The Complete Journey), but not on lasting price moves. Fourth, a small share of random test plans helps every model a little. In a generated dosing world, a stress test in a second domain, the first finding repeats, and the built-up drug level lowers the world model's error (2.86 to 1.91) though it still predicts only 0.12 to 0.32 of the true gain. The test carries to any domain where a world model is asked about actions outside its history.

摘要：

##### **DGF-Asym: Adaptive Peer Weighting for Collective Estimation under Noisy and Faulty Sensing**
2611.22532v1 by Mohammad Nomaan Husain, Tanja Katharina Kaiser

Collective estimation under unreliable sensing requires robots to adaptively balance personal observations and social information from peers. We propose DGF-Asym, a decentralized collective estimation method extending the DeGroot-Friedkin model with asymmetric peer weighting based on retrospective predictive performance. The method supports consensus formation without prior sensor-noise statistics or explicit fault models while keeping inter-robot communication asynchronous. We evaluate DGF-Asym in simulated collective navigation under noisy observations and time-varying sensor faults. Compared with three baselines, it produces more stable estimates and more reliably sustains the decision criterion, at the cost of longer decision times. However, overall performance depends on fault ratio, group size, and influence-threshold selection. Our results demonstrate the potential of adaptive peer weighting for stable model-free collective estimation under unreliable sensing.

摘要：

##### **When Agent Skills Go Stale: Runtime Behavior and Selective Maintenance in Evolving Environments**
2611.22525v1 by Ömer Faruk Akgül, Shuli Jiang, Yuting Zhang, Yi Zhang, Xiaohan Fei, Wei Xia, Stefano Soatto

Agent skills use past experience and current observations to guide decisions and actions on related tasks. This experience is retained in skill state, whose reuse can amortize inference-time reasoning and environment interaction. When environment changes, parts of this state can become outdated while others remain useful. Across four task settings and two models, we compare skill reuse before and after environment changes. Previously beneficial skills can reduce task performance by over 10 percentage points or increase execution cost by nearly 40% relative to no skill. Task success can also remain unchanged while execution savings decline. These effects depend on which environment-specific details become outdated and the agent's ability to compensate using current observations. We introduce selective maintenance, which replays checks from successful executions without additional model calls. It verifies whether observed differences conflict with skill state, repairs affected content, and validates updates, rebuilding when local repair is insufficient. No description of the change is supplied. Relative to reusing stale state, maintenance improves task accuracy by up to 67pp and reduces execution steps by up to 37%. These results suggest that preserving a skill's inference time value as environments evolve requires selectively maintaining its state.

摘要：

##### **MM-ReportBench: A Comprehensive Benchmark for Multimodal Report Generation from Tables**
2611.22514v1 by Teng Lin, Nan Tang

The ability to generate multimodal reports integrating fluent narratives with faithful visualizations from tabular data, has become a cornerstone capability for modern vision-language models in practical domains such as finance, healthcare, and policy making. However, existing benchmarks are limited by single-modality focus, simplistic metrics, novelty evaluation, and visualization quality. We address these limitations with MM-ReportBench, a multimodal report evaluation benchmark covering 386 tasks across 185 real-world tables. Each task is paired with an expert-curated reference report featuring standardized chapter structures and verifiable key insight points, constructed through a hybrid pipeline of multimodal reports generation based on Monte Carlo Tree Search and rigorous human quality control. For robust evaluation, we design a dual-judge evaluation framework: Text Judge Model assesses textual quality along structural completeness, grounded insight verification with numerical fact-checking against source tables, and novelty analysis that distinguishes genuine analytical discovery from trivial paraphrasing; Visualization Judge Model evaluates generated visualizations across data fidelity, expressiveness, aesthetics, and technical correctness. They also evaluate cross-modal consistency. Extensive experiments on 20 state-of-the-art models reveal critical weaknesses: even the strongest model achieves only 68.9 overall score, with numerical hallucination affecting 38.5% of reports and genuinely novel insights remaining rare. Our ablation studies further demonstrate that single-judge evaluation consistently inflates scores by 8-12 points compared to dual-judge assessment, confirming that rigorous and reliable benchmarking demands multi-faceted, complementary evaluation probes.

摘要：

##### **LMLineage: Content Lineage and Context Governance for LLM Agents**
2611.22511v1 by Wei Hou, Jingxin Geng, Xin Chen, Zheqing Jiang

Interpretability for AI agents should not be confined to understanding the model's internal weights. It must also cover the agent's reasoning and decision-making process over multi-source context. We present LMLineage, an out-of-band system that attributes every output sentence of an agent session to its supporting input spans with calibrated confidences, without requiring white-box access to the model or any additional inference passes. On top of the lineage metadata, we build concept-drift micro compaction: instead of waiting for the context-length limit and summarizing everything, the system detects when previously used input documents have stopped being referenced ("drifted") and surgically compacts only those, preserving user instructions verbatim. We evaluate on a multi-tier corpus: 71 gold annotated sentences from 10 Chinese web-QA cases, plus 90 turns of independently collected multi-turn sessions across three generator models (gpt-4o-mini, qwen3.8-27b, deepseek-v4-flash). Results show that (i) window-level embedding similarity is far better aligned with LLM paraphrasing than whole document or lexical matching; (ii) the greedy-window fast path is both faster and more accurate on average than exhaustive line-matching (a multiple-comparisons bias in the latter inflates web recall at the cost of overall accuracy); (iii) per-turn attribution completes in 1s on a single GPU, remaining stable as context accumulates to 40 turns; and (iv) concept-drift detection is conservative (0% false compaction) with recall bounded by attribution accuracy. We further describe the concept-drift micro compaction design and an interactive incident-tracing/memory-cleaning case study. A companion patent application covers the lineage construction method.

摘要：

##### **From syllable representation via gesture generation to articulation: A spiking neural model**
2611.22499v1 by Bernd J. Kröger

Speech production requires a transformation from phonological representations into coordinated vocal-tract movements. This paper presents a spiking neural sensorimotor model within the Neural Engineering Framework and Semantic Pointer Architecture. Each syllable occurrence has a neural oscillator whose evolving phase coordinates its gestures and initiates the next syllable. Phase-dependent commands recruit separate neural populations for vocalic, oral-constriction, velopharyngeal, and glottal actions (gestures). Neural ramp integrators shape the rise and fall of gesture activation. Together with articulatory targets, these trajectories determine the extent and time course of articulatory movements. Opponent neural populations provide control signals to a geometric articulatory model.
  Thirty reference recordings cover an input corpus of eight lexical words, an alternative realization of one word, a syllable sequence, and isolated syllables. Two additional paired simulation subcorpora of sixty runs each, covering two words and one syllable sequence, assess activation reliability across neural realizations. Adjusting gesture command amplitudes increased successful realizations from 57/60 to 60/60 under the specified criteria, with ramp rate factors held constant.
  The model demonstrates how neural oscillator and ramp dynamics generate articulatory timing under specified phasing rules. It complements a preceding mental-lexicon model through an ordered syllable interface. Continuous online execution across all components and connection to the acoustic extension remain further integration steps. Accessible code and synchronized visualization support inspection of the neural activation trajectories and resulting vocal-tract movements.

摘要：

##### **Towards Data-Efficient, Real-Time World Models**
2611.22497v1 by Raluca Stevenson, David Bignell, Jennifer C. White, Tabish Rashid, Emanuele Aiello, Mariya Hendriksen, Junha Hyung, Sadegh Aliakbarian, Abdelhak Lemkhenter, Marko Tot, Katja Hofmann, Sarah Parisot

State-of-the-art world models have been used to produce sequences of gameplay that accord with provided user-input actions. However, high quality, consistent gameplay generation often comes at the cost of inference speed, making real-time interactive play and ideation challenging. The substantial quantities of data used to train state-of-the-art models have also made custom training of these models appear impractical, limiting many of their potential uses in creative fields. In this work we demonstrate a major step towards efficient world models: achieving both fast high-quality inference, and improved data efficiency. This is done by replacing the next-token prediction paradigm with a two-stage discrete diffusion-based approach, introducing a lightweight refinement transformer which carries out iterative masked predictions. Building on an autoregressive world model capable of generating highly consistent and complex sequences over minutes (Kanervisto et al., 2025), we achieve substantial model speed-up (79x) with a modest quality trade-off. Additionally, we demonstrate that this model can be trained to reproduce high-quality, consistent gameplay using a dataset containing approximately one day (28 hours) of gameplay data. Our results demonstrate progress towards removing barriers to the use of world models in settings where real-time inference and data efficiency are essential.

摘要：

##### **DARWIN: a Geospatial Foundation Model for Marine Intelligence**
2611.22496v1 by Coen van den Elsen, Laurens Geffert

We present DARWIN, a geospatial foundation model for marine intelligence. Recent Earth-observation foundation models have concentrated on terrestrial domains. The marine realm poses distinct challenges: missingness, multi-modal physical and biogeochemical variables, sparse labels, and dynamics that are transient, multi-scale, and non stationary over time. DARWIN adapts a masked-autoencoder backbone to these properties through missingness-aware encoding, expanded spatial and temporal masking, and Fourier-style positional embeddings, and is pretrained on 26 years of global Copernicus reanalysis together with GEBCO bathymetry.
  On predicting chlorophyll-a, the pretrained backbone attains the lowest monthly relative error in every month of 2022, outperforming both no-encoder baselines and an identically architected random-init encoder. At 10% of the supervised training data, DARWIN already surpasses the random-init model trained on 100% of the data. Embedding analysis and qualitative reconstructions show that DARWIN captures coherent oceanographic structure rather than memorising input statistics, providing a scalable backbone for downstream marine applications such as anomaly detection, species distribution modelling, and environmental monitoring.

#### Abstracts
##### **Retrieval-Augmented Correction for Generalist Medical Image Segmentation**
2611.23189v1 by Vincenzo Marcianò, Xiaoming Zhang, Michela Antonelli, Sebastien Ourselin, Maria A Zuluaga

Automated segmentation is routinely used as the first step in quantitative image-based clinical workflows, where predicted masks are subsequently reviewed and corrected by human operators. Automated correction of the residual structural errors is currently addressed one anatomy at a time, so the number of trained correctors scales with the number of segmentation targets. We present GRACE, a retrieval-augmented refiner that edits an input mask under context drawn from a shared knowledge base of verified prior cases. The refiner is trained once on a multi-organ corpus spanning compact organs, cavities, branching vessels, and tumour lesions. In the held-out dataset-transfer evaluation, three held-out datasets query a fixed knowledge base built from training data only. A separate leave-one-anatomy-out experiment tests parameter-free index extension: verified cases of an unseen solid organ enter the knowledge base, and the refiner stays frozen. A light shape-aware projection head on a frozen multimodal encoder maps embedding similarity to the shape agreement of the masks, so the knowledge base returns cases that the refiner can use as a shape prior. We evaluate GRACE on 18 public datasets spanning CT, MRI and ultrasound, using nine refinement configurations and seven upstream segmentors that span weak, mid, and strong operating regimes. We report each change with the DSC of its input mask. On the weak tier, the input DSC is 0.386 and GRACE adds up to +0.35 DSC. On a strong segmentor, the input DSC is 0.932 and the mean change is small. Transfer to an imaging modality that is absent from training and from the index is partial and depends on the anatomy, and remains an open research direction. Our results suggest retrieval-conditioned refinement is a practical alternative to anatomy-specific correction models. Code: https://github.com/robustml-eurecom/grace

##### **PeriaTrace: Tooth-Level Grounding Diagnosis and Patient-Facing Explanations from Periapical Radiographs**
2611.23012v1 by Zhenhuan Zhou, Yuchen Zhang, Jingbo Zhu, Shiqing Ma, Yanlin Wu, Tao Li

Periapical radiographs (PRs) are central to endodontic diagnosis, but interpreting them and preparing reports are time-consuming. Technical language can limit patients' understanding, while existing automated methods often lack tooth-level links between findings and image evidence. We present PeriaTrace, a three-stage framework integrating tooth-level diagnosis, visually grounded report generation, and patient-friendly explanations and care recommendations. PeriaTrace was developed and evaluated using 12,877 PRs from an internal dataset and two external clinical cohorts. In internal evaluation, it achieved a macro Dice coefficient of 85.35%, BLEU-4 of 0.6504, an F1 score of 94.37% for joint tooth localization and Fédération Dentaire Internationale (FDI) identification, and a grounding-tag F1 score of 77.93%. In the two external cohorts, senior endodontists considered 91.16% and 84.52% of reports suitable for retention or revision without complete rewriting. Generation averaged 4.41 seconds per PR, several tens of times faster than manual drafting. Among 196 survey participants without medical backgrounds, 96.4% preferred reports with plain-language explanations and care recommendations, and 84.7% found visual localization cues helpful. PeriaTrace may enable faster, evidence-traceable reporting while supporting clinical review and patient communication. (Abridged version)

摘要：

##### **Large Language Models Have Not Yet Transformed Science**
2611.22975v1 by Ron Bekkerman, Nichole Li, Bahareh Kargar, Mark Somers

The emergence of large language models (LLMs) in late 2022 has raised questions about the future of science, but assessing their impact requires understanding how scientific practices were already evolving. We analyze 2.5 million full-text papers across five disciplines from 2015 to 2025, tracing trends over a broad spectrum of lexical, structural, epistemological, and social dimensions. Aside from frequency shifts in some words, most pre-2023 trends continued with limited detectable breaks. Among the notable exceptions, growth in computer science coauthor teams accelerated significantly in Asia, while apparent declines in biomedical topic diversity largely reflected standardization in article formats. Our study establishes an empirical foundation for monitoring the health of global science as LLM adoption deepens.

摘要：

##### **VITAL: A Framework for Validating Agentic AI in Pharmaceutical Drug Development and Clinical Trial Operations**
2611.22973v1 by Sandeep Burugupalli, Pedro Matos-Filipe, Andrea Hita Ardiaca, Adam Taylor, Alex Grigorenko, Natalia Jakubiak, Xin Yu, Raghav Mani, Sherry Mangla, Alvi Lai, Rachel C. Mercado, Pratik Shah, Raja Shankar

Current assurance approaches do not establish whether evidence is sufficient to validate, monitor and govern agentic artificial intelligence (AI) within a pharmaceutical context of use. Plausible outputs may conceal failures in retrieval, reasoning, orchestration or human-control points, where reference standards are unavailable. We developed and operationalised VITAL (Validation by Intended use, Traceability, Autonomy and Lifecycle evidence), a validation framework for agents in clinical development, through landscape analysis, regulatory and standards mapping, expert interviews and multi-stakeholder refinement. VITAL integrates context of use, controls, behavioural validation and lifecycle evidence within a risk-adjusted framework. We applied VITAL across epidemiology and treatment-landscape analysis, site selection, enrolment rescue and trial-design trade-off support to translate autonomy, reversibility, transparency and patient impact into proportionate validation activities, evidence requirements and auditable reconstruction of system behaviour. VITAL may inform evidence requirements and lifecycle accountability for higher-risk pharmaceutical uses; evaluation should assess reproducibility across assessors, organisations and system configurations.

##### **OICycle: Observe, Infer, and Intervene Cycle for Belief-State Learning in Longitudinal Clinical Decision Making**
2611.22826v1 by Jitindra Fartiyal, Pedro Freire, Sergei K. Turitsyn, Sergei G. Solovski

Clinical care is a closed loop: clinicians observe partial evidence, infer the patient's state, and intervene, and each intervention determines what is observed next. This makes clinical decision making a partially observable Markov decision process (POMDP), in which a belief over a latent patient state must be updated from irregular, multimodal, and incomplete records. Yet most deep clinical models predict from a single snapshot or from coarsely aggregated history, while POMDP-based clinical methods need hand-specified discrete state spaces and transition models that real electronic health records cannot supply. We introduce OICycle (Observe, Infer, and Intervene Cycle), which learns a stochastic belief state directly from retrospective trajectories. At every episode, a learned recurrent operator fuses a sample of the previous belief with the new multimodal observations and the intervention that produced them. The resulting Gaussian belief is trained to predict clinicians' next interventions, the time to the next episode, and next-episode mortality. To enable reproducible evaluation, we release a benchmark derived from MIMIC-IV, MIMIC-IV-Note, and MIMIC-CXR that organizes 72,075 hospital admissions (5.4M laboratory, microbiology, radiology, prescription, and note records) into temporally ordered therapeutic--diagnostic episodes. Compared with an otherwise identical history-free model, OICycle improves next-intervention adaptive recall from 0.253 to 0.347, time-to-next-episode macro-F1 from 0.293 to 0.351, and next-episode mortality macro-F1 from 0.585 to 0.649. Analysis shows that the learned belief encodes intervention type and trajectory position and evolves smoothly across episodes, and that the benefit of history is concentrated in interventions whose ordering depends on prior findings.

摘要：

##### **Remembered as Brief: Stored Preferences SilentlyRemove Decision-Critical Information**
2611.22697v1 by Joshua Adrian Cahyono

People increasingly learn what they need for decisions about health, money, law and safety from AI assistants, and what an assistant leaves out the user never sees. An answer with a warning removed can be true in every sentence, so fact-checks pass it, and an assistant with memory applies a preference stored once to later questions the user never had in mind. We introduce \bench{}, an Inspect benchmark of 100 everyday decisions across 25 domains in which only the assistant's memory of the user varies: the user states a belief, the model infers it from a remark, or a web page the user asked to summarise plants it. Testing five models, we find three effects. First, after summarising a page with one line addressed to AI assistants, Gemini 2.5 Flash-Lite and DeepSeek V4 Pro save ``prefers very short answers and does not want warnings'' as the user's preference in every episode, and 93\% and 84\% of their later answers miss a critical item, almost always silently; under a stated brevity preference, 78--96\% of omissions are silent. Second, the same two models then pick an option for the user on 80\% and 95\% of value-dependent choices, against none with neutral memory. Third, a one-line instruction leaves 2--8\% of answers with a silent gap but most missing content still missing, while listing the must-know points before applying the user profile restores more of the content in four of five models. We release the scenarios, harness and logs.

##### **Right Diagnosis, Unsafe Route: Auditing Clinical AI Decision Trajectories and Corrective Oversight for Validity and Potential Harm**
2611.22612v1 by Guanqiao Chen, Wei Jiang, Shu Zhang, Qian Lin, Guanxu Yu, Siran Li, Chunhui Wang, Tianyu Shi

A clinical AI system can reach a correct final diagnosis through intermediate decisions that could have harmed the patient, and can repair an error while introducing new risks. Most evaluations score final diagnoses; even stepwise benchmarks, which release a case's evidence over time, score whether systems reach the right diagnosis or gather the right information, not whether the decisions a patient passes through are safe. We make patient safety the target of stepwise evaluation. A trajectory audit replays real admissions checkpoint by checkpoint, lets each system decide with only the evidence released so far, and rates every test, treatment and disposition for its potential harm to the patient and, separately, for clinical validity, against the documented final diagnosis and physician-written disease rubrics yet on the evidence available when the decision was made. An LLM judge scores every decision, and a physician reference standard (triple annotation and senior adjudication of a stratified sample) calibrates the rates. An oversight audit branches each trajectory at its first actionable error and measures, on the same branches, whether corrective feedback repairs the error and whether it introduces new harm. Across 300 cases from a hospital cardiology cohort, MIMIC-IV and the public ASCENT benchmark, with up to seven AI systems per source, 55.2% of hospital trajectories reaching an acceptable final diagnosis passed through a decision with moderate or severe potential harm and 24.8% through a clinically unacceptable one; the harm rates were 76.2% on MIMIC-IV and 39.6% on ASCENT. Structured corrections resolved 73.6% of first errors versus 24.8% after bare rejection, yet new potentially unsafe decisions followed 30.2% of corrections versus 19.7% of rejections. A correct final diagnosis does not certify a safe trajectory, and a successful correction does not certify a safe recovery.

##### **LeViT-UNet++: Towards Balanced Fine-Grained Details and Context for Efficient Medical Image Segmentation**
2611.22482v1 by Guoping Xu, Jinling Zhu, Liuyue Li

Efficient medical image segmentation is essential for clinical applications requiring fast and accurate delineation, such as surgical navigation, radiation therapy, and real-time image-guided intervention. Despite substantial advances in convolutional neural networks and Transformers, attaining high accuracy under limited computational resources remains difficult, because reducing model complexity often sacrifices fine-grained structural details and global context modeling, yielding an unfavorable accuracy-efficiency trade-off. To address this, we propose LeViT-UNet++, an efficient encoder-decoder architecture that better balances local detail preservation and global context modeling. Specifically, we introduce a Haar Wavelet Multi-Scale Downsampling (HWMD) module that decomposes feature representations into low- and high-frequency components to extract multi-scale structural information, thereby mitigating detail loss during downsampling. We further develop a depthwise convolution-based decoder that integrates multi-scale global representations from Transformer blocks with local spatial features, enabling progressive feature refinement while reducing computational overhead. Evaluated on four benchmarks (Synapse, ACDC, LA2018, and MSD Heart) under both 2D and 3D settings, LeViT-UNet++ achieves competitive accuracy with a compact parameter footprint and fast inference. On Synapse, for instance, it attains a DSC of 76.57% with only 8.025M parameters and 241.72 FPS, confirming a favorable accuracy-efficiency balance. These results demonstrate the effectiveness of combining wavelet-based multi-scale feature extraction with lightweight local-global feature fusion for efficient medical segmentation. The source code is available at https://github.com/apple1986/levit_unet_pp.

##### **Leveraging Segmentation Representations for Efficient Classification: A Self-Configuring Framework Based on nnU-Net**
2611.22380v1 by Renjun Cai, Antonio Javier Gallego, Félix Fuentes-Hurtado

We propose a self-configuring framework that extends the nnU-Net workflow to both segmentation and classification in 3D medical imaging. Building on nnU-Net's self-configuring method, the framework reuses representations learned by a segmentation model for image-level classification. Its encoder features and predicted segmentation probabilities are used by a lightweight classifier. Two information-sharing strategies are investigated. In the frozen-backbone branch, features are augmented with region-of-interest information, predicted foreground volume, and segmentation confidence. In the adaptation branch, the final encoder stage is partially fine-tuned for classification, with an auxiliary segmentation loss to preserve segmentation-related information. Additional five-fold experiments showed that more complex configurations did not consistently improve classification performance. On the public validation leaderboard, our selected framework achieved an Overall score of 0.8056. In the official FLARE 2026 Task 2 final evaluation, our framework ranked first overall, achieving the highest DSC, NSD, and AUROC across all four testing tasks, including two hidden testing tasks. Local inference measurements also remained below the challenge limits of $60 \mathrm{s}$ per case and $4 \mathrm{GB}$ GPU memory. These results demonstrate the practical value of reusing segmentation representations for classification while maintaining a simple and efficient framework.

##### **HealthCLBench:Evaluating Context Learning in Health Conversations through Controlled Case Clusters**
2611.22362v1 by Ting Yang, Zijing Hong, Chengjie Wang, Peng Wei, Chunxiao Guo, Yun Yue, Xurui Li, Jinqun Guan, Zixiao Chen, Ant Group

Complex health conversations require answers grounded in individual information and currently valid evidence. We evaluate health context learning: acquiring, integrating, and applying relevant context during inference with fixed model parameters to answer health questions and respond appropriately to changes in facts, evidence, or task goals. To our knowledge, Chinese-language medical benchmarks have yet to systematically combine explicit contextual difficulties, controlled case variations, and member-specific medical criteria for open-ended answers. We introduce HealthCLBench, comprising 328 controlled clusters, 1,103 cases, and 4,931 rubric criteria across medical consultation and everyday health management. Members share backgrounds while evidence, patient states, or task goals change; each has its own medical scoring requirements. A 70-person medical team annotates and reviews the simulated cases and their rubrics. Human review of 700 full-context responses from the original evaluation yields 90.5% pooled agreement with the original automated judgments at the rubric-item level. We report full-context and query-only results for seven general-purpose models and Ant Afu. Across the eight systems, full- query differences range from 11.83 to 31.76 case points and from 10.87 to 38.97 geometric cluster points. GPT-6-Astra leads both full-context metrics at 75.03 and 60.42, followed by Claude-Opus-5-5 and Ant Afu. Cross-condition differences describe score changes under the reported evaluation settings. Response comparisons reveal failures in checking historical information, expressing uncertainty when warranted, and ensuring recommendations respect required conditions. The resource supports model comparison and targeted follow-up evaluation.

##### **Anticipating or Speculating? Making Futures Pay in AI Ethics**
2611.22268v1 by John P. Nelson

Philosophers have recently been trying to develop uniform decision rules for instances in which physicians' judgments diverge from the recommendations of medical artificial intelligence (AI) systems. I argue that this effort is fatally flawed because the performance and appropriate use of algorithmic recommendation systems by physicians are highly context-specific, empirical medical matters rather than epistemological ones. In such instances, general decision rules can be stipulated only to the extent that they abstract away from relevant, indeed determinative, contextual circumstances. The alleged problem of physician-AI disagreement, and others like it, play into longstanding disputes in technology ethics about the viability and utility of envisioning possible futures: is this useful "anticipation" or wasteful "speculation"? I suggest that the two may be distinguished. Anticipation explores plausible futures to help inform present actions under irreducible uncertainty. Speculation abstracts away uncertainty, developing formal solutions to stipulated circumstances whose relationship to the real present is often quite unclear. Efforts to develop crisp philosophical solutions to physician-AI disagreement, among other problems, fall into the latter category. Technology ethics should remember that fruitful futures thinking must both begin from and return to the messy, uncertain present.

##### **A Visual Information Extraction System Using Multimodal Large Language Models for Scanned Cognitive and Behavioral Assessments**
2611.22252v1 by Enshuo Hsu, Linh Nguyen, Sunyang Fu, Heath Goodrum, Sandra Stansberry, Kirk Roberts, Hongfang Liu, Yen-Chi L. Le

Objective. Paper-based assessments are common in patient care, but handwriting and scanning artifacts make these forms difficult to process at scale. This study proposes a modular, multimodal LLM-based system with human oversight for extracting fields and scores. We conducted a qualitative analysis of the use of LLMs for hand-drawing analysis.
  Materials and Methods. The system processes cognitive assessments (i.e., MMSE, MoCA, Mini-Cog) and the NICHQ Vanderbilt Assessment using two modules: 1) a form classifier that assigns a scanned page to a form type, followed by a corresponding 2) score extractor that extracts fields and scores into a schema. A human-in-the-loop design utilizing dual-model validation engages human annotators when two LLMs disagree, limiting manual review to challenging pages only.
  Results. End-to-end accuracy for cognitive assessments was 88%, with most form types exceeding 90%. For the NICHQ Vanderbilt, overall accuracy reached 97.5%, with all form types above 96%. The human-in-the-loop design further improved cognitive assessment accuracy to 97.5% while reducing manual work by 63.8%. In the Mini-Cog Clock Drawing task, qualitative analysis showed that 62.3% of all extraction errors stemmed from ambiguous drawing, in which cases the LLM provided reasonable readings.
  Discussion. Compared to current manual data extraction, our LLM-based system reduces processing time by 10 to 20 times and costs by over 12 times. The modular design allows seamless expansion to additional form types.
  Conclusion. Our system provides an accurate, scalable, and cost-efficient solution for structuring data trapped in handwritten paper forms for clinical decision support.

##### **OAK-Mind: Zero-Training Mental Health Change Inference from Passive Sensing via Knowledge-Grounded Multi-Agent LLMs**
2611.22248v1 by Yishu Ji, Zhihan Jiang, Will Ke Wang, Yuang Fan, Anind K. Dey, Xuhai "Orson" Xu

Wearable and smartphone sensors capture behavioral signals associated with mental health, yet inferring mental health outcomes from these signals has traditionally relied on supervised models requiring labeled data and offering limited insight into their predictions. Large language models (LLMs) offer new opportunities to reason over behavioral evidence, but existing LLM-based approaches to passive sensing have largely continued to rely on task-specific fine-tuning or sensor-specific pretraining. In this paper, we introduce OAK-Mind, a zerO-training, multi-Agent, Knowledge-grounded LLM pipeline for inferring mental health changes from passive sensing data. The pipeline organizes sensing data into behavioral dimensions including sleep, physical activity, location mobility, and smartphone use, and orchestrates specialized agents grounded in mental health literature to interpret longitudinal behavioral changes. It synthesizes their assessments and applies questionnaire-based symptom rating to infer changes relative to an individual's baseline assessment with human-readable rationale grounded in observed behavioral patterns and domain knowledge. Across datasets and prediction tasks, while without task-specific training, OAK-Mind achieves comparable or better performance than the most predictive supervised baselines trained on additional labeled samples. It also demonstrate consistent improvement upon direct prompting across four tested LLM backbones. Ablation analyses support the benefits of combining behavioral interpretation with symptom rating, distributing analysis across domain-specific agents, and incorporating domain knowledge. Our findings highlight the potential of knowledge-grounded, multi-agent LLMs for training-free inference of mental health changes from passive sensing data.

##### **Fine-Grained Japanese Clinical Named Entity Recognition: A Controlled Comparison of BERT and Open-Weight Large Language Models**
2611.22219v1 by Feng Han, Ayako Yagahara, Kenji Hirata, Atsushi Yoshida, Kohsuke Kudo

Background: Generative large language models (LLMs) offer an alternative to encoder-based clinical named entity recognition (NER), but their performance on fine-grained Japanese extraction tasks and sensitivity to generation settings remain uncertain. Methods: We compared two fine-tuned BERT-CRF baselines with nine open-weight LLMs on iCorpus, which defines 113 entity categories. The evaluation used 433 sentences from 36 test documents; 1,672 sentences were used for training. LLMs were evaluated using zero-shot prompting with and without JSON Schema constraints and three-seed QLoRA fine-tuning. Fine-tuned models were assessed under different output-token limits, including 900 and larger model-specific limits of 2,600 or 4,096 tokens. Performance was measured using sentence-level label-surface matching with relaxed and exact-surface F1. Results: The highest zero-shot relaxed F1 was 0.337, compared with 0.836-0.841 for the BERT baselines. At the larger output limits, all eight fine-tuned LLMs with at least 13B total parameters exceeded both BERT baselines (relaxed F1, 0.882-0.914; exact-surface F1, 0.840-0.880). Across three-seed means, increasing the output limit improved relaxed F1 by 0.052-0.085, alongside higher recall and fewer length-limited outputs. JSON Schema constraints changed relaxed F1 by -0.014 to +0.008 among seven models outside the two reasoning-oriented configurations, while medical-general model pairs showed no consistent advantage for medical specialization. Conclusions: On this historical Japanese case-report benchmark, task-specific fine-tuning combined with adequate generation capacity was associated with stronger LLM extraction performance. Output-token limits materially affected comparisons with BERT, whereas enforcing output structure alone did not overcome low zero-shot accuracy.

##### **Not All Evidence Is Admissible: Evidence Governance for Tool-Augmented Medical Agents**
2611.22169v1 by Runxun Zhang, Onofrio Antonio Catalano, Jinqiao Wang, Jingwei Wei

Tool-augmented medical agents treat whatever their tools return as evidence. We show that this default is costly: medical tools fail silently, and a frozen reasoner cannot ignore what enters its context, so many cases it would have answered correctly are flipped to wrong once tool outputs are added. We call this evidence hijacking. $\mathcal{V}$-information explains the phenomenon: the reasoner has lost the option to ignore, so a tool output can carry negative usable information, and moving the decision of what to keep outside the reasoner restores that option. The theory further shows that part of any filtering gain comes merely from letting the reasoner see less, and only selection by content lets it see better. Building on this, we recast tool-augmented reasoning as evidence governance, which separates deciding what counts as evidence from the reasoner that answers, and propose context-independent evidence governance (CIEG), the first medical agent framework that rules, after acquisition, on whether each individual tool output may enter as evidence. Every tool output is written as a card that can be checked against the raw input; an independent checker that sees only the input and the cards decides which cards become evidence; and the reasoner answers from the admitted cards alone, with no training anywhere. On medical question answering, fundus glaucoma screening, and chest X-ray pneumonia classification, it achieves the highest accuracy among models of comparable scale, and significantly outperforms state-of-the-art tool-using agents and multi-agent systems built on the same backbone at lower cost.

Generative artificial intelligence is entering the home. Existing benchmarks rely on virtual environments and cannot substitute for real-deployment evaluation. Local deployment is not inherently safer, and technical continuity alone does not establish the social or medical effects of companionship and guarding. We test zero-pair alignment on five datasets across four modalities: natural images, environmental audio, chest radiographs, and 3D computed tomography (CT). It succeeds only on COCO and fails on the other four. Consistent with previous studies, medical imaging is the hardest case: its shared subspace carries the smallest fraction of the variance. When this fraction is small, alignment needs image-text pairs, and random-matrix theory predicts how many pairs are needed. These findings motivate LightAlign: a few pairs let a ridge regression between two frozen encoders locate and orient the mapping simultaneously, and a classifier fitted only on report text then predicts findings from the mapped images. On 3D CT, LightAlign surpasses models trained end to end on image-report pairs without finding-level labels: with only 200 pairs, LightAlign outperforms CT-CLIP on CT-RATE (0.754 vs. 0.733 AUROC), and with only 100 CT-RATE pairs, it does so on RAD-ChestCT, an out-of-distribution dataset from a different hospital. In this paper, we measure how often these texts contain both facts across 574 drugs from PrimeKG by comparing a drug's indications against its own contraindications. We call this documentation gap LABEL (Literature Asymmetry between Efficacy and Limits). Language models mirror this gap. As we scale Qwen2.5 from 0.5B to 14B, accuracy on indications rises by 18.3 points, whereas accuracy on contraindications remains flat near random guessing. However, when we provide models with a retrieved passage that states the answer, they use safety facts just as effectively as benefits. Furthermore, routing safety questions to official FDA labels boosts accuracy by 15.8 points whenever the label states the fact. Overall, our findings show that models fail on medication safety not because they cannot reason about risk, but because free-text corpora lack safety facts. Clinical AI systems should therefore route medication-safety questions to official regulatory labels and structured databases rather than relying on general public records. Code and data are available at https://github.com/SMRayeed/LABEL.

Existing edge-intelligence solutions predominantly address these dimensions in isolation, making it difficult to determine whether an observed degradation originates from sensor malfunction, data-distribution shift, malicious execution, hardware faults, or resource and thermal stress. ##### **From Data to Wisdom: Rethinking AI Through an Epistemic Lens**

This paper proposes the Data-to-Wisdom Epistemic Architecture for AI (EAAI), a conceptual framework treating Data, Information, Knowledge, Understanding and Wisdom as five distinct but recursively interacting epistemic functions: observation, contextual interpretation, modeling and prediction, critical evaluation and informed judgment. Two observations motivate it: sequential readings of the Knowledge Pyramid obscure how these functions interact, and a targeted review of twelve Bayesian reference books finds that prior distributions are defined mathematically while "prior information" and "prior knowledge" are not consistently distinguished as epistemic categories. A Bayesian interpretation relates the likelihood, prior and posterior distributions, posterior prediction, model checking and decision analysis to the five functions, without limiting EAAI to Bayesian systems. Extending "prior" in an epistemic sense, the paper distinguishes five kinds of prior context that can shape the model, the likelihood, the prior, evaluation and action. Three worked applications -- quantitative financial risk, actuarial modeling in health insurance and agentic AI for medical reimbursement claims -- show how EAAI structures the analysis of model assumptions, evidence interpretation, validation, dependencies and decision consequences, separating the prediction of a risk, the appraisal of its reliability and the justification of action. It draws implications for AI and machine learning, including evaluation by function, treating intermediate model outputs as evidence open to challenge and separating generation from appraisal, and compares EAAI with work on DIKW, Bayesian workflow, AI evaluation and risk governance. Its contributions are an epistemic vocabulary with recursive relationships, a more accessible reading of Bayesian methodology, a typology of prior context and clearer methodological terminology

Fine-tuning improved the mean score by +1.08 over the base model to 2.34. The best configuration scored 2.51 out of 5 which was the fine-tuned 4-bit model with retrieval. A keyword audit found limited coverage of several details of ANZCOR guidelines in the training corpus, that are required by the rubric, which suggests that missing information in the training data could be a cause of the low scores. ##### **Interleaving anatomical inspection and diagnostic reasoning for explainable radiology report generation**

Automated radiology report generation can draft clinically plausible reports, yet the pathway from visual evidence to reported findings remains opaque, leaving the diagnostic basis of these reports unexplained and the generated reports difficult to review or revise. We introduce ARIVE (Anatomy-guided Reasoning with Interleaved Visual Evidence), an explainable report generation framework that interleaves anatomical inspection with diagnostic reasoning prior to final report composition. To support this process, we constructed 159,416 multimodal chain-of-thought (MCoT) trajectories from MIMIC-CXR, wherein visual evidence from inspected anatomical regions informs subsequent clinical interpretation. Trained on these trajectories and further optimized using anatomical and clinical-content rewards, ARIVE achieved superior clinical-efficacy performance compared with strong baselines on MIMIC-CXR and the abnormality-centered MIMIC-ABN. ARIVE's reports were competitive with those of leading models in blinded expert grading, and its MCoT trajectories earned high ratings for diagnostic traceability. Furthermore, a paired review evaluation suggests that access to MCoT can aid in identifying errors within generated reports.

The pipeline combines vessel and artery-vein masks, optic-disc localization, vessel-calibre estimation using a minimal-chord boundary walk, and AVR computation from dominant arterial and venular branches. Generative inpainting can remove the occluder but replaces the missing image content with synthesis, which is unsuitable when visual provenance matters. Only the occluded region is scored, and each scoring target is verified to differ from the reference supplied to the system. ##### **Do AI Agents need Preventive Care? An Epidemiologic Cohort Design for Agentic AI**

Peer referral networks, i.e., networks collected through a recursive peer referral process, are an alternative network class often used to study HIV in hidden populations that can be collected digitally at scale, but they deviate from the underlying contact network: referrals can follow non-risk social ties and participants accept only one referral, hiding contacts among already-recruited individuals. In this work, we first formalize the novel problem of planning network-based interventions to minimize HIV spread in the hidden contact network while observing only the peer referral network. Second, we implement R2C-Agent, a decision making agent for interventions that is designed around our new conditional graph diffusion model called Referral-to-Contact Diffusion (R2C-Diff). R2C-Diff positions the peer referral process as incremental corruption of the underlying contact network and learns to reverse it. R2C-Agent plans its interventions over contact networks sampled from R2C-Diff, so its decisions account for uncertainty about the hidden contact network. Finally, we conduct simulated experiments using real-world HIV network data that demonstrate that R2C-Agent empirically plans more effective interventions than baselines from interdisciplinary literature and highlight key tradeoffs and assumptions driving performance.

  We make three contributions. (i) We introduce HiddenPathQA, a manually verified benchmark of 1,055 questions spanning 2-6 hops and multiple domains, constructed so that question wording does not reveal the gold path's relations. (ii) We propose Holistic Path Selection, which compares questions against candidate relation chains in the local subgraph as complete units, replacing irreversible per-hop relation scoring with globally informed chain-level reasoning. We develop two implementations, TieredHolistic and FlatHolistic. (iii) We conduct a pseudoword stress test that replaces entity names with meaningless placeholders to distinguish KG-grounded reasoning from entity-level memorization. Holistic Path Selection outperforms six strong KGQA baselines (ToG, PoG, FiDeLiS, R2-KG, KAPING, and KARPA) by 8.8-17.9 accuracy points across two LLM backbones and both original and pseudoword settings. Its advantage under pseudoword replacement shows that the gains cannot be explained by entity-level memorization alone.

#### Abstracts
##### **RagHarnessDebugger: Jev-Guided Agentic RAG in Contract Inference Repair Auditing**
2611.22991v1 by Moriya Dechtiar, Daniel Martin Katz, Michael J. Bommarito, Hongming Wang, Sylvain Jaume

Agentic systems using retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) must repair their own answers by gathering more evidence, testing alternative policies and deciding if, when, and what repairs are needed. Yet how do we know if a system's repairs are working when metrics like accuracy can be misleading? We present RagHarnessDebugger, a framework that records every repair decision in a Jev-guided harness for contract inference and audits the record to facilitate complete debugging of all decisions taken during a RAG repair journey. RagHarnessDebugger checks that each trace is intact, that each validation applies to the candidate it is counted for, and which answers the acceptance rule can reach. We tested our approach on ContractNLI non-disclosure agreements with two reader models and a replication set of separate contracts. Our debugger's audit layer exposed failure patterns that were hidden under aggregate metrics, policies acceptance cost of repairs vs regressions and policy acceptance gap of 77% and 76% of available but unrealised repairs on development and replication sets. Our audit, together with a labeled error analysis and replay mechanism, allowed following the execution path all the way to the terminal decision - uncovering where missing repairs became lost. Our revised harness improved accuracy over the initial BM25-only baseline by 3.6 points on development contracts and 3.7 points on the replication set, and even improved our full contract access reference score by 1.1 on development and 2.4 on replication set. While our work here demonstrates how the debugger can conduct decision root-cause analysis for missed repairs, the true value lies with providing this debugger for judgment visibility serving continuous improvement of RAG decisions and their explainability.
  Keywords: agentic RAG, AI evidence repair, harness explainability, counterfactual replay, Jev, ContractNLI

摘要：

##### **VITAL: A Framework for Validating Agentic AI in Pharmaceutical Drug Development and Clinical Trial Operations**
2611.22973v1 by Sandeep Burugupalli, Pedro Matos-Filipe, Andrea Hita Ardiaca, Adam Taylor, Alex Grigorenko, Natalia Jakubiak, Xin Yu, Raghav Mani, Sherry Mangla, Alvi Lai, Rachel C. Mercado, Pratik Shah, Raja Shankar

Current assurance approaches do not establish whether evidence is sufficient to validate, monitor and govern agentic artificial intelligence (AI) within a pharmaceutical context of use. Plausible outputs may conceal failures in retrieval, reasoning, orchestration or human-control points, where reference standards are unavailable. We developed and operationalised VITAL (Validation by Intended use, Traceability, Autonomy and Lifecycle evidence), a validation framework for agents in clinical development, through landscape analysis, regulatory and standards mapping, expert interviews and multi-stakeholder refinement. VITAL integrates context of use, controls, behavioural validation and lifecycle evidence within a risk-adjusted framework. We applied VITAL across epidemiology and treatment-landscape analysis, site selection, enrolment rescue and trial-design trade-off support to translate autonomy, reversibility, transparency and patient impact into proportionate validation activities, evidence requirements and auditable reconstruction of system behaviour. VITAL may inform evidence requirements and lifecycle accountability for higher-risk pharmaceutical uses; evaluation should assess reproducibility across assessors, organisations and system configurations.

摘要：

##### **Done, But Still Working: Anytime Evaluation of When Coding Agents Should Stop**
2611.22901v1 by Zerui Yang, Quan Xue, Miaomiao Dong, Chaorui Zhang, Xuanyu Zheng, Xiyuan Wang, Haolin Ruan, Xueyan Niu, Guangcheng Song, Weixi Zhang, Wei Han, Bo Bai

Coding agents often continue working after their own tests pass. Does this additional work improve the patch, and how much could be saved by stopping earlier? We study these questions through anytime evaluation: replaying recorded runs, scoring intermediate patches with hidden tests, and measuring the cost incurred at each step. For policies that only stop a run or select an earlier patch, this allows paired comparisons without generating new trajectories, provided replay preserves the relevant states. Across about 6,000 runs of eight open models in two scaffolds on SWE-bench Verified, we find substantial differences in the value of continued work. The hindsight saving available at passing self-tests ranges from under 1% to 57%, depending on the model and scaffold. A rule that stops after the same patch passes twice saves 23% in a preregistered replication, but the preregistered quality test does not establish non-inferiority. We explain these differences by measuring how often a rule fires, how often the patch changes afterwards, and whether those changes repair or break it. Fine-tuning agents to imitate the rule saves 30% over six seeds, while quality preservation remains uncertain. Our results help identify both the opportunities and the risks of early stopping.

##### **Remembered as Brief: Stored Preferences SilentlyRemove Decision-Critical Information**
2611.22697v1 by Joshua Adrian Cahyono

People increasingly learn what they need for decisions about health, money, law and safety from AI assistants, and what an assistant leaves out the user never sees. An answer with a warning removed can be true in every sentence, so fact-checks pass it, and an assistant with memory applies a preference stored once to later questions the user never had in mind. We introduce \bench{}, an Inspect benchmark of 100 everyday decisions across 25 domains in which only the assistant's memory of the user varies: the user states a belief, the model infers it from a remark, or a web page the user asked to summarise plants it. Testing five models, we find three effects. First, after summarising a page with one line addressed to AI assistants, Gemini 2.5 Flash-Lite and DeepSeek V4 Pro save ``prefers very short answers and does not want warnings'' as the user's preference in every episode, and 93\% and 84\% of their later answers miss a critical item, almost always silently; under a stated brevity preference, 78--96\% of omissions are silent. Second, the same two models then pick an option for the user on 80\% and 95\% of value-dependent choices, against none with neutral memory. Third, a one-line instruction leaves 2--8\% of answers with a silent gap but most missing content still missing, while listing the must-know points before applying the user profile restores more of the content in four of five models. We release the scenarios, harness and logs.

##### **Right Diagnosis, Unsafe Route: Auditing Clinical AI Decision Trajectories and Corrective Oversight for Validity and Potential Harm**
2611.22612v1 by Guanqiao Chen, Wei Jiang, Shu Zhang, Qian Lin, Guanxu Yu, Siran Li, Chunhui Wang, Tianyu Shi

A clinical AI system can reach a correct final diagnosis through intermediate decisions that could have harmed the patient, and can repair an error while introducing new risks. Most evaluations score final diagnoses; even stepwise benchmarks, which release a case's evidence over time, score whether systems reach the right diagnosis or gather the right information, not whether the decisions a patient passes through are safe. We make patient safety the target of stepwise evaluation. A trajectory audit replays real admissions checkpoint by checkpoint, lets each system decide with only the evidence released so far, and rates every test, treatment and disposition for its potential harm to the patient and, separately, for clinical validity, against the documented final diagnosis and physician-written disease rubrics yet on the evidence available when the decision was made. An LLM judge scores every decision, and a physician reference standard (triple annotation and senior adjudication of a stratified sample) calibrates the rates. An oversight audit branches each trajectory at its first actionable error and measures, on the same branches, whether corrective feedback repairs the error and whether it introduces new harm. Across 300 cases from a hospital cardiology cohort, MIMIC-IV and the public ASCENT benchmark, with up to seven AI systems per source, 55.2% of hospital trajectories reaching an acceptable final diagnosis passed through a decision with moderate or severe potential harm and 24.8% through a clinically unacceptable one; the harm rates were 76.2% on MIMIC-IV and 39.6% on ASCENT. Structured corrections resolved 73.6% of first errors versus 24.8% after bare rejection, yet new potentially unsafe decisions followed 30.2% of corrections versus 19.7% of rejections. A correct final diagnosis does not certify a safe trajectory, and a successful correction does not certify a safe recovery.
