##### **JET: Just-Enough Exploration Tuning for Efficient Tool-Calling Agents in Repository-Level Question Answering**
2611.22539v1 by Yoonho Choi, Taehyeon Han, Seonghyeon Lee

In repository-level question answering (QA), an agent explores a code repository with tools and should answer a question once enough information is gathered. Nevertheless, we find that LLM agents often continue to invoke tools even after the gathered information is already sufficient to answer. We call this phenomenon over-exploration. Moreover, even though existing methods improve task performance through supervised fine-tuning (SFT) on expert trajectories, SFT also propagates the over-exploration in the trajectories to the agent. To mitigate over-exploration, we propose Just-Enough Exploration Tuning (JET), a fine-tuning method that trains the agent to answer once the gathered information is sufficient. JET truncates each training trajectory at the step where the gathered information becomes sufficient, inserts a rationale that explains why the agent can answer at that step, and fine-tunes the agent with a higher loss weight on the rationale. Experiments on two repository-level QA benchmarks with two open-weight 8B models demonstrate that JET reduces tool calls by 11 to 33% on self-generated trajectories while largely preserving the score. On expert trajectories, JET makes 18 to 28% fewer tool calls than SFT while retaining most of the score gain of SFT. In both cases, JET also lowers the inference cost.

##### **Right Labels, Wrong Reasons: Signed Feature Attribution for Multi-Label Explainability**
2611.22515v1 by Kenton White

When a multi-label classifier assigns the right labels, it may do so for the wrong reasons. Diffuse feature combinations may vary across classifier families and lack a dominant feature. This paper proposes FLEX (Fisher LDA Explainer), a global surrogate that fits a single direct multi-label Fisher LDA over the full binary label matrix, producing signed feature attribution lists for each label in a single coherent coordinate system. Across the evaluated benchmarks and classifier families, FLEX reproduces the black box's predictions with consistently high fidelity. Pooling covariance across labels outperforms a per-label alternative on most datasets, with the advantage growing largest where labels are numerous and positive examples are scarce. FLEX's fidelity-complexity Pareto curve is monotonically increasing, giving practitioners a predictable path to higher-fidelity explanations as more features are used, and its in-sample fidelity matches or leads Binary Relevance alternatives once a modest feature budget is allowed. The magnitude of each discriminant weight vector bounds its label's separability, flagging labels that may be predicted correctly for the wrong reasons. The evaluated classifiers largely agree on which predictions to make, but reach those decisions through different feature subsets when no single feature dominates.

##### **Anticipating or Speculating? Making Futures Pay in AI Ethics**
2611.22268v1 by John P. Nelson

Philosophers have recently been trying to develop uniform decision rules for instances in which physicians' judgments diverge from the recommendations of medical artificial intelligence (AI) systems. I argue that this effort is fatally flawed because the performance and appropriate use of algorithmic recommendation systems by physicians are highly context-specific, empirical medical matters rather than epistemological ones. In such instances, general decision rules can be stipulated only to the extent that they abstract away from relevant, indeed determinative, contextual circumstances. The alleged problem of physician-AI disagreement, and others like it, play into longstanding disputes in technology ethics about the viability and utility of envisioning possible futures: is this useful "anticipation" or wasteful "speculation"? I suggest that the two may be distinguished. Anticipation explores plausible futures to help inform present actions under irreducible uncertainty. Speculation abstracts away uncertainty, developing formal solutions to stipulated circumstances whose relationship to the real present is often quite unclear. Efforts to develop crisp philosophical solutions to physician-AI disagreement, among other problems, fall into the latter category. Technology ethics should remember that fruitful futures thinking must both begin from and return to the messy, uncertain present.

##### **Not All Evidence Is Admissible: Evidence Governance for Tool-Augmented Medical Agents**
2611.22169v1 by Runxun Zhang, Onofrio Antonio Catalano, Jinqiao Wang, Jingwei Wei

Tool-augmented medical agents treat whatever their tools return as evidence. We show that this default is costly: medical tools fail silently, and a frozen reasoner cannot ignore what enters its context, so many cases it would have answered correctly are flipped to wrong once tool outputs are added. We call this evidence hijacking. $\mathcal{V}$-information explains the phenomenon: the reasoner has lost the option to ignore, so a tool output can carry negative usable information, and moving the decision of what to keep outside the reasoner restores that option. The theory further shows that part of any filtering gain comes merely from letting the reasoner see less, and only selection by content lets it see better. Building on this, we recast tool-augmented reasoning as evidence governance, which separates deciding what counts as evidence from the reasoner that answers, and propose context-independent evidence governance (CIEG), the first medical agent framework that rules, after acquisition, on whether each individual tool output may enter as evidence. Every tool output is written as a card that can be checked against the raw input; an independent checker that sees only the input and the cards decides which cards become evidence; and the reasoner answers from the admitted cards alone, with no training anywhere. On medical question answering, fundus glaucoma screening, and chest X-ray pneumonia classification, it achieves the highest accuracy among models of comparable scale, and significantly outperforms state-of-the-art tool-using agents and multi-agent systems built on the same backbone at lower cost.

##### **Long-Context Generation Is a Sampling Problem (When You Turn Up the Heat)**
2611.22148v1 by Allen G. Roush, Minh Nhat Nguyen, Ravid Shwartz-Ziv, Judah Goldfeder, Sanjay Basu

Open-weight language models are widely observed to get worse the longer they generate. Often, attempts to fix this involve more training. At high temperature, The sampler, i.e. the rule that picks the next token out of the model's probabilities, decides whether long and agentic generation survives and/or thrives. We test 29 open-weight models from 1B to 122B parameters on ten established benchmarks plus needle retrieval out to one million tokens, covering SWE-bench Verified coding agents, long chain-of-thought reasoning, long-form writing, long structured output and long-context reading. Samplers that keep their pool of candidate tokens small and steady as temperature rises don't lose accuracy. Samplers that let in low-probability tokens fall apart. At temperature 2, coding agents running top-p, top-k or Top-H resolve 0 of 100 GitHub issues, while the same agents running top-nsigma resolve 46 to 62 and win every issue the two samplers disagree on, on three Qwen3.5 models. Over all benchmarks top-nsigma keeps a median 100% of its temperature-1 score where top-p keeps 26%. Counting how many tokens each sampler lets through explains why. In one Llama-3.1-8B story, going from temperature 1 to 2 grows top-p's pool from 4 tokens to 51,845 while top-nsigma stays at 3. Across 133 combinations of sampler setting and temperature, the typical pool size predicts judged quality (Spearman correlation of -0.77), and when we pick the pool at temperature 1 and only apply temperature afterwards, Top-H, min-p and p-less stop collapsing. At the default temperature of 1 most samplers perform about the same, We release all code and data to the public.

##### **On Formal Counterfactual Explanations**
2611.22010v1 by Alexey Ignatiev, Peter J. Stuckey, Joao Marques-Silva

Counterfactual explanations are often seen as counterfactual conditionals under closest-world semantics, requiring one to reason over all minimally distant counterexamples. In contrast, formal XAI typically relies on feature-based abductive explanations (AXps) and contrastive explanations (CXps), which admit efficient computation and a minimal hitting-set duality. This paper bridges these perspectives by (i) characterizing closest-world counterfactual explanations explicitly as a disjunctive normal form over all minimally distant counterexample instances, and (ii) introducing conjunctive counterfactual explanations (CFXps), a subclass of counterfactual antecedents restricted to conjunctions of feature literals that guarantee prediction change. The paper establishes structural results connecting CXps, AXps and CFXps: every CXp induces an AXp for its witness instance, and every CXp together with a witness AXp induces a CFXp whose support is precisely determined by that AXp. These theoretical results yield a modular approach for computing CFXps using existing AXp/CXp machinery. Experiments on a representative set of benchmarks using tree ensembles and neural networks show that CFXp extraction is practically efficient. Compared with popular point-based counterfactual explainers, CFXps generally require no more feature changes and can guarantee the counterfactual prediction beyond a single instance.

##### **SimAI: Mechanistic User Simulation through Psychological Network Models**
2611.21808v1 by Cheng Li, Adhyyan Narang, Jiayi Yuan, John F. Buss, Jina Suh, Devon Sandel-Fernandez, Yulia Tsvetkov, Adam M. Kuczynski, Tim Althoff

Realistic user simulation enables the study of human interactions when direct experimentation is costly or potentially harmful. However, existing simulators largely overlook the psychological mechanisms that drive user behavior. We introduce SimAI, a framework for mechanistic user simulation that models how psychological factors interact to shape user responses. To overcome the challenge of supervising open-ended behavior, we propose a graph reconstruction objective that provides graph-level rewards based on how well simulated conversations reflect the underlying psychological relationships. To support this approach, we construct PSYCHE, a dataset of 18,576 profile-network pairs derived from 516 psychotherapy cases. Automatic evaluations show that SimAI achieves greater simulation and behavioral diversity than existing patient simulators while maintaining strong profile alignment. In an interactive study with 30 mental-health professionals and trainees, SimAI receives the highest average ratings for conversational authenticity, realistic help-seeking behavior, and clinical training utility. Beyond simulation quality, SimAI enables realistic clinical training and uncovers safety failures in prolonged AI interactions with vulnerable users. Code is available at https://github.com/behavioral-data/ANGEL-UserSim.

##### **Beyond Reconstruction: A Unified Framework for Trustworthy and Explainable EEG Artifact Removal Evaluation**
2611.21781v1 by Phat Lam, Hung Nguyen, Lam Pham, Huy Phan, Dat Tran, Ian McLoughlin, Roozbeh Razavi-Far, Hung Cao

Deep-learning methods have achieved strong performance in EEG artifact removal, but evaluation remains dominated by reconstruction metrics such as error, correlation, and signal-to-noise ratio. Although these metrics assess similarity to clean EEG, they do not establish whether models effectively suppress artifacts, preserve neural activity, or provide reliable explanations. To address these limitations, we propose TriX-EEG, a three-level framework for trustworthy and explainable EEG artifact removal. Level 1, Signal Validity (SV), evaluates reconstruction quality and neural band-power preservation. Level 2, Removal Faithfulness (RF), assesses the removed component through artifact error and residual artifact/neural leakage. Level 3, Explanation Reliability (ER), examines model behavior through temporal alignment, causal relevance, and stability. We evaluate TriX-EEG using nine published neural denoisers across three public benchmarks derived from EEGDenoiseNet and the PhysioNet Motion Artifact Contaminated EEG Dataset, covering EOG, EMG, and motion artifacts. Results show that reconstruction metrics alone are insufficient for trustworthy denoiser assessment: RF complements it by revealing whether errors stem from residual artifact or suppressed neural activity, while ER captures distinct aspects of explanation behavior, supporting joint evaluation of three aspects within a unified framework rather than reliance on any single factor. External validation on an independent EEG/EOG dataset and the BCI Competition IV-2a motor-imagery dataset further demonstrates broadly consistent rankings under dataset shift. Higher-ranked denoisers generally preserve more task-relevant information or cause less downstream performance degradation. Overall, TriX-EEG provides a comprehensive basis for EEG denoiser assessment, encouraging models that are not only accurate but also faithful and explainable.

##### **Severe but Harmless: Stress-Testing Shift Severity for Frozen Time-Series Forecasters**
2611.21562v1 by Korede R. Bishi, Dominic Okonkwo, Adetayo Okunoye

Zero-shot time-series foundation models (TSFMs) have become attractive alternatives to task-specific models because they handle diverse downstream tasks, including anomaly detection and forecasting, without per-series training. However, time-series data undergo distribution shifts, and the dissimilarity between two regimes is commonly scored with statistical measures such as Wasserstein-1, where a higher score is read as a more severe shift. In systematic experiments using three established severity measures on nine regime shifts from three standard traffic datasets, we show that these measures do not reliably predict degradation for instance-normalized TSFMs. The regimes rated most severe are not the regimes that degrade the models most, and degradation reaches 29% in the lowest-scoring regime. In most cases, the audit shows no positive relationship between measured severity and model degradation. We provide an equivariance-based stress-testing analysis that explains why severity readings can become decoupled from model degradation.

##### **Optimistic Verifiers Get Hacked: Reward Hacking Under an Exact Process Oracle**
2611.21538v1 by Arunkumar V, Manoranjan Gandhudi, Gangadharan G. R., Arun Prakash, S. Senthilkumar

Reinforcement learning from verifiable rewards inherits whatever its verifier gets wrong. Outside mathematics and code the verifier is a proxy, a rubric, a learned judge or a hand-written checker, and its distance from the true objective is never measured, so a rising reward cannot be told apart from a hack. We build a setting where the true reward is exactly computable at the process level: executable authorization policies over 124,416 contexts admit an oracle that lists every minimal sufficient reason for every decision. We compile these policies into exact process rewards, degrade the verifier along seven realistic axes (outcome-only, sufficiency, recall-only, extraction noise, coarse taxonomy, stale policy, LLM judge), and train against each while the exact verifier scores every rollout. Four phenotypes appear. Optimistic verifiers are hacked by padding: the sufficiency proxy ends at 0.940 while true faithfulness falls from 0.731 to 0.667 on 96 held-out episodes. Outcome-only verifiers saturate and stop teaching, noise dilutes, and ranking-preserving gaps do no harm. A model-free audit of the verifier, its selection error and gap sign, together with a short saturation pilot, anticipates which phenotype a run will produce. Across nine configurations spanning two frameworks, two model vendors, two adapter sizes, three precisions, two initialisations and two seeds, the phenotypes recur, sufficiency damage is positive in all 10 replication cells and beyond two standard errors in 7, and optimistic gaps remain the steeper branch. The damage magnitude does not replicate, and five pre-registered attempts to explain it are reported as they fell. We release the testbed, the training instrumentation, and 14 real public policies (Cedar, OPA, Gatekeeper) ported to executable oracles.

##### **Programmable Semantic Interventions: Learning Causal Carriers from Behavioral Programs**
2611.21307v1 by Paolo Muratore, Mackenzie Weygandt Mathis

Causal abstraction explains a frozen network by aligning its activations with the variables of a simpler, interpretable model, and checking that interventions on the two agree. Finding such an alignment currently requires a counterfactual dataset: for every pair of prompts, the answer the network ought to give once the variable has been changed. In practice, those labels are produced by an executable algorithm for the behavior, written before studying the model supposed to implement it. However, the criterion these methods optimize only leverages the answer an intervention should produce. Here, we develop an approach that lets the investigator define that relationship directly: the answers the variable can produce, and which of them an intervention should yield. We call this interface a semantic program and introduce Programmable Semantic Interventions (PSI): a low-rank activation subspace learned so that interchanging it changes the model's answer as the program declares, with the expected answers read from the frozen model itself. On the MIB benchmark PSI performs on par with supervised alignment at matched data and training budget, without curated pairs, and it needs no ground-truth labels; while on natural science questions with gold labels withheld, it tracks the model's own answers better than a subspace trained on gold labels. The declaration also fixes a coordinate system with known semantics, enabling donor-free steering: jointly training PSI for both localization and control realizes 94.8% of held-out requests for answers the investigator chooses, without the need for donor prompts.

2611.21306v1 by Sven Jacobs, Lukas Berning, Natalie Kiesler

##### **Language-model agents' beliefs can be manipulated via truthful evidence in open channel**
2611.21235v1 by Jinwei Hu, Qisong He, Xinmiao Huang, Youcheng Sun, Yi Dong, Xiaowei Huang

Language-model agents' beliefs can be manipulated through open channels such as social media, even when the information they receive is truthful. When agents fill gaps between fragments with self-induced relationships, they may accept those relationships as established facts and pass the resulting rumours to others. Drawing on cognitive theory of narrative understanding and film theory of montage, we introduce Generative Montage to operationalize this failure, termed narrative overfitting, by coordinating agents to arrange truthful fragments into misleading narratives. Across 1,090 rumour targets from six Twitter events, agents often accepted rumours overconfidently, with overall acceptance exceeding 65 percent in twelve of fourteen models. Acceptance remained above 50 percent after majority voting or review of the same evidence, showing how errors can reach downstream decisions. To explain this susceptibility, fragment interventions identify posts that influence belief within complete narratives. Synthetic controls then show that similar response patterns arise from valid evidence, separating belief influence from evidential support. This distinction motivates Grounded Monitor, a proof-of-concept neuro-symbolic runtime monitor that checks direct evidential support to guide agents' judgements, improving decision accuracy by 5.6 to 29.0 percentage points across original and anonymized settings. These findings expose a cognitive route to rumour amplification and support monitoring inferred relationships before agent-generated conclusions guide public decisions.

##### **A Generative AI-Driven Architecture for Whole-Home Intelligent Services: Edge Models, Memory Management, and Household Applications**
2611.21232v1 by Mo Zhou

Generative artificial intelligence is entering the home. Cloud-based assistants face governance limitations in data transmission, continuous availability, and the reliability of physical actions, which are especially salient in households where devices remain online, data accumulate over time, and commands trigger real-world actions. Using the home local server as a unifying organizing axis, this article develops a design-oriented review framework. Sources from arXiv, CNKI, ACL Anthology, and official documentation were searched through November 2026 and coded into five evidence categories: reviews, system prototypes, benchmarks, HCI and eldercare studies, and official documentation. System designs were mapped onto shared architectural modules, identifying nine recurrent responsibilities of the home local server. Generative models support perceptual interpretation, intent understanding, and candidate planning, while deterministic validation, knowledge-graph constraints, and authorization leases confine candidate actions to an inspectable space. Home control, emotional companionship, and intelligent guarding can be compared within a common framework: they share state and memory but differ in latency, false positive and negative rates, proactivity, and permissions. Existing benchmarks rely on virtual environments and cannot substitute for real-deployment evaluation. Local deployment is not inherently safer, and technical continuity alone does not establish the social or medical effects of companionship and guarding. The article proposes a five-dimensional evaluation framework covering architectural capability, system resources, privacy, safety and reliability, and human experience, together with six design principles and a future research agenda for local-first intelligent home systems.

##### **InclusionMed: Benchmarking AI for Inclusive Healthcare**
2611.21218v1 by  The InclusionMed Collaboration

Advancing medical AI for inclusive healthcare requires evaluation across diverse healthcare roles, tasks, and care settings, but benchmark coverage remains uneven. We introduce InclusionMed, an open medical AI evaluation initiative for inclusive healthcare. The InclusionMed Atlas organizes benchmarks across three healthcare roles and three AI capabilities. For the first release, we selected four public benchmarks from 388 candidates through structured audits and independent reproduction. With InclusionMed-DxFiles, our private diagnostic reasoning benchmark, the Atlas contains five benchmarks covering three of its nine role--capability cells. We evaluated frontier large language models on the Atlas and aggregated their results into the InclusionMed Index, with overall scores ranging from 17.5 to 49.1 out of 100. The Index also reports scores by healthcare role and AI capability, showing that a model's rank varied with the role or capability considered. A within-benchmark analysis using task-level tags showed that the ten highest-ranked models in the Index all scored higher on patient communication than on clinical decision-making. Models with similar Index scores also differed substantially in estimated inference cost. These findings highlight the need for evaluation covering more diverse healthcare needs and for context-specific model selection. Through open collaboration and community contributions, InclusionMed seeks to ensure that diverse healthcare needs worldwide shape how medical AI progress is defined and measured.

##### **Linglong Agent Service: A Runtime for Centralized Agent Reasoning and Distributed Execution**
2611.21129v1 by Hua Zhang, Min Jiang

LLM agents can use remote tools and cloud sandboxes, but integrating reasoning, session state, and heterogeneous execution sites remains a runtime design problem. We present Linglong Agent Service (agentsvc), a self-hosted HTTP service and embeddable Python runtime that treats execution site as a configurable dimension alongside model, tools, and context. A parent service retains inference orchestration, tool descriptions, and conversation history; each inference request selects one child environment for file, shell, terminal, and MCP operations. Direct HTTP and child-initiated WebSocket tunnels carry the same application interfaces, including portable request context and centrally managed updates. The normal core path requires no third-party Python packages, with an optional Windows pseudo-terminal dependency. We further present online_debugger as the process-scale special case of execution-site separation: an instrumented program submits exception evidence, executes session-scoped inspection tools inside the incident process, and resumes the same diagnostic session. Its default prompt requires failing-then-passing reproduction evidence for source changes, while runtime patch activation and failed-call recovery remain unimplemented. We explain the design through constrained expected-utility reasoning, compare concrete mechanisms with Hermes Agent, Pi, Codex, and Claude Code, and report implementation and regression-test evidence. The current evidence establishes executable contracts, not performance advantages, repair accuracy, or production recovery. The source code is open source under the MIT license at https://github.com/mz24cn/agents.

##### **System 1 agents: a method for local, reliable and fast agentic AI**
2611.21053v1 by Eleftherios Tzanis, Michail E. Klontzas

Agentic artificial intelligence could automate analyses of sensitive clinical data, but current agents depend on large language models (LLMs). Cloud LLMs expose patient data. During inference, some of these transformations are dynamically constructed from the current input: previously acquired transformations are first adapted and then applied. At the mechanical-structural level, this dynamic processing is realized through output-weight interconnections, whereby outputs of some networks determine the weights of others. The resulting mode of processing, which we call Sequence-level Interactive Dynamic Parallel Processing (SIDPP), provides a framework for explaining strong prompt sensitivity and opens research directions concerning interpretability, control, alternative AI architectures, neuromorphic systems, and possible biological realizations of dynamic processing.

AI holds significant potential to enhance educational practices through the facilitation of personalized and adaptive learning, automation of assessment procedures, and assistance with administrative tasks. However, the opacity of AI-based decision-making in education (especially with deep learning and generative AI models) can hamper decision-making, especially when outcomes appear unjustified or incorrect. In this chapter, we argue that three key principles (uncertainty, explainability, and human responsibility) are essential for the legitimate use of AI in educational settings. Acknowledging the inherent uncertainties in AI-based decisions, analogous to those in human decision-making, is fundamental. Explainability is critical for ensuring that the functioning and outcomes of AI applications are transparent and comprehensible to both educators and learners. Furthermore, human responsibility is crucial, particularly in human decision-making, which remains indispensable across all stages of AI development and deployment. By examining these three principles, this chapter provides a comprehensive guide for stakeholders seeking to integrate AI thoughtfully and effectively into the educational system.

##### **From Data to Wisdom: Rethinking AI Through an Epistemic Lens**

This paper proposes the Data-to-Wisdom Epistemic Architecture for AI (EAAI), a conceptual framework treating Data, Information, Knowledge, Understanding and Wisdom as five distinct but recursively interacting epistemic functions: observation, contextual interpretation, modeling and prediction, critical evaluation and informed judgment. Two observations motivate it: sequential readings of the Knowledge Pyramid obscure how these functions interact, and a targeted review of twelve Bayesian reference books finds that prior distributions are defined mathematically while "prior information" and "prior knowledge" are not consistently distinguished as epistemic categories. A Bayesian interpretation relates the likelihood, prior and posterior distributions, posterior prediction, model checking and decision analysis to the five functions, without limiting EAAI to Bayesian systems. Extending "prior" in an epistemic sense, the paper distinguishes five kinds of prior context that can shape the model, the likelihood, the prior, evaluation and action. Three worked applications -- quantitative financial risk, actuarial modeling in health insurance and agentic AI for medical reimbursement claims -- show how EAAI structures the analysis of model assumptions, evidence interpretation, validation, dependencies and decision consequences, separating the prediction of a risk, the appraisal of its reliability and the justification of action. It draws implications for AI and machine learning, including evaluation by function, treating intermediate model outputs as evidence open to challenge and separating generation from appraisal, and compares EAAI with work on DIKW, Bayesian workflow, AI evaluation and risk governance. Its contributions are an epistemic vocabulary with recursive relationships, a more accessible reading of Bayesian methodology, a typology of prior context and clearer methodological terminology

The uniqueness claim is conditional; for $k<d_z-1$, where $d_z$ is the number of world states, this limit makes its premise unsatisfiable, so the claim has no content there. The experiments that motivate it leave the assumption: a caption sees only part of an image, and the paper states no experiment meets its hypotheses exactly. The theorem does not reach those experiments, and for $k<d_z-1$ its idealized same-state limit is vacuous. One of the paper's four quantitative results survives: a gap-free lower bound. Its bound vanishes only at perfect scene-explained fraction; it bounds alignment error but cannot certify good-view alignment. In exploratory, non-preregistered analyses, Study 1 had four open-weight LLMs assess six acute-pain vignettes in every combination of four races and two sexes, with identity stated or cued only by a name (two names per group), comparing each identity with a White male reference on the same case and sampling seed. In Study 2, 24 configurations of 17 models, including OpenAI's GPT-4.1 mini and gpt-oss, Anthropic's Claude Haiku 4.5, Google's Gemini 2.5 Flash, Meta's Llama, DeepSeek and Qwen, with reasoning on and off and at two precisions, answered the same vignettes and the 50 clinician-written Q-Pain cases. In this single-prompt, JSON-constrained task, no difference survived correction in Study 1, none of 1,386 comparisons survived the false discovery rate in Study 2, and every pain-severity difference was equivalent to zero within one point on a 0-10 scale (within a quarter point for 237 of 238 Q-Pain comparisons). Opioid decisions were bounded less precisely (Study 1 could not exclude differences of about 20 percentage points), so they show no detected difference, not proof of none. The few differences surviving correction within a model favoured Black patients. Models differed far more from one another and across their own settings than between patients (the reference patient received the high opioid dose in 6% to 93% of responses), although serving software, provider and prompt format also differed. Fairness audits of clinical AI should use several names per group, pair comparisons on the seed, report the noise floor and test each deployed configuration.

##### **Do AI Agents need Preventive Care? An Epidemiologic Cohort Design for Agentic AI**
2611.13333v1 by Shu Jiang, Graham A. Colditz

As AI agents increasingly perform tasks autonomously across sectors, including healthcare and biomedical research, understanding how their behaviors develop and influence outcomes over time becomes increasingly important. Existing evaluations largely focus on model capabilities and performance on predefined tasks. Here, we propose an epidemiologic cohort design for studying agentic AI, drawing on principles established in human population studies. The framework treats individual agents as members of a cohort, their underlying model as genetic makeup, accumulated work as their time scale, and naturally occurring behaviors as exposures that may influence subsequent outcomes. By following agents longitudinally, this approach enables investigation of behavioral determinants of adverse events, estimation of their potential preventable burden under causal assumptions, and characterization of behavioral changes over an agent's work life. We illustrate the design through two prospective observational cohorts examining curiosity and probing as a safety question, and data checking and analytical accuracy as a reliability question. Together, the framework and illustrative studies provide a generalizable blueprint for designing epidemiologic cohorts of AI agents.

摘要：

##### **Decisions by Discrimination: Differential Evidence for Medical Reasoning**
2611.13233v1 by Yunpeng Xiao, Yuzhang Xie, Junhao Huang, Carl Yang, Xiao Hu, Kai Shu

Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly studied for clinical decision support, and medical question answering provides a testbed for evaluating this capability. In particular, medical multiple-choice questions (MCQs) reflect a core challenge in clinical decision-making: plausible options often share overlapping evidence, while only a few decisive factors distinguish the correct option from incorrect ones. However, existing retrieval-based and reasoning-oriented methods mainly focus on supplying broadly relevant knowledge or eliciting reasoning paths, rather than identifying the compact differential evidence that separates confusing alternatives. In this work, we present a retrieval-grounded differential evidence framework for medical MCQs. Our method extracts option-specific pieces of differential evidence and organizes them into concise evidence prompts for answer selection. This design better aligns external knowledge with differential reasoning while reducing irrelevant context. Experiments show consistent accuracy gains across multiple datasets and target LLMs. Ablation studies further show that our framework outperforms both directly using retrieved evidence chunks and closed-source LLM-generated general hints, and that its improvements are not solely explained by answer-proximal guidance, but are consistent with differential evidence serving as a compact aid for reasoning over competing alternatives. Our findings also offer insights for medical reasoning and other decision-making settings with multiple possible choices.

摘要：

##### **XAI-Driven Confidence Estimation for OOD-Resilient Runtime Prediction**
2611.13199v1 by Mahmoud Sanad, Mohammed Bakr Sikal, Osama Abboud, Heba Khdr, Jörg Henkel

Machine learning-based resource management (RM) models degrade under out-of-distribution (OOD) inputs at runtime, yet existing approaches in the literature rely on a single global error margin that is overly conservative and fails to distinguish reliablepredictions from catastrophic failures. We propose Lightweight Runtime EXplainability (LEX), the first post-hoc XAI framework for input-specific runtime confidence estimation in ML-based RM. LEX clusters the embeddings of the training input space using a Gaussian Mixture Model and associates per-cluster prediction error bounds to input samples, enabling fine-grained runtime confidence signals without retraining. Evaluated on a state-of-the-art LSTM-based IPS forecasting model on Intel Alder Lake, across 34 benchmark applications, LEX achieves ~94% containment on unseen workloads and produces ~17% tighter per-input confidence bounds than global baselines. With its negligible runtime overhead, LEX advances the field by providing explainable and reliable input-specific confidence signals of ML predictions, thereby making ML-based resource management resilient to OOD at runtime.

##### **One Temperature to Rule Them All?**
2611.13077v1 by Aviv Orly, Ori Shem-Ur, Yaron Oz

Inference-time sampling is a central mechanism for improving large language models, with temperature controlling the tradeoff between reliability and diversity. While recent work suggests that varying temperature across samples can improve multi-sample inference, simple fixed-temperature schemes remain the standard in practice. We investigate this discrepancy in the canonical pass@K setting, asking when temperature diversity is useful, and when a single tuned temperature suffices. To this end, we analyze per-question success probabilities across multiple models and datasets, tracking how they vary with sampling temperature. We identify several consistent properties of these profiles, including that harder questions are less likely to share efficient sampling temperatures, although this trend is only logarithmic in hardness. We then derive an efficient method for evaluating the theoretically optimal multi-temperature allocation scheme, and show that although such a scheme improves performance, its gains over single-temperature baselines are typically modest when temperature is tuned as a function of the sampling budget K. We explain this phenomenon through mathematical mechanisms that we call dulling effects, and show how they ensure the robustness of temperature choices across different question distributions. Our results clarify when multi-temperature sampling is useful, and how temperatures should ideally be chosen.

##### **LUCQUE: A lung cancer screening dataset from Québec for reliable AI evaluation**
2611.12817v1 by Johanna Topalis, Yannick Lemaréchal, Gabriel Couture, Hanife Ersay, Loran Nas, Balthasar Schachtner, Bastian Oliver Sabel, Michael Ingrisch, Philippe Després, Katharina Jeblick

BACKGROUND: AI-based tools are expected to play a key role in lung cancer screening (LCS). The evaluation of these tools often relied on private datasets or fragmented public datasets with inconsistent availability of clinical data and expert nodule annotations. This has hindered reproducibility, comparability, and independent assessment of AI tools. To address these gaps, we present LUCQUE, a novel, large, comprehensively annotated dataset derived from LDCT lung cancer screening in Québec, which is accessible for reliable AI evaluation.
  METHODS: LUCQUE was curated and is hosted on PARADIM, a digital infrastructure supporting DICOM data de-identification, annotation, long-term storage and analysis. LDCT scans and clinical information were combined to a curated collection and complemented by voxel-level, expert-derived pulmonary nodule and cyst segmentations, and Lung-RADS-based lesion characteristics. Key dataset characteristics and statistics were reported, including participant demographics, imaging parameters, radiological findings, and segmented lesion characteristics. Dataset analyses were conducted according to PARADIM's model-to-data approach, in which user code is executed on the platform such that data remains on premise.
  RESULTS: LUCQUE currently comprises 1,678 LDCT baseline studies from 1,678 participants. Most studies were classified as Lung-RADS 2. A total of 1,776 pulmonary nodules and 60 cysts were annotated, with a median (interquartile range) volume of $89\,(56,\,159)\,\mathrm{mm}^3$ and $1,384\,(589,\,3793)\,\mathrm{mm}^3$ respectively.
  CONCLUSIONS: Compared to publicly available LCS datasets, LUCQUE currently represents the largest accessible annotated real-world LCS dataset. Access to LUCQUE is provided via a model-to-data approach, enabling privacy-preserving, reproducible, and independent real-world evaluation of AI tools in LCS.

摘要：

##### **LogCDM: Interpretable Log Anomaly Detection via Behavioral Graph Drift and Adaptive Signal Fusion**
2611.12775v1 by Suryansh Talukdar, Taha Sarwat, Jin Soung Yoo

Log-based anomaly detection is increasingly dominated by large pretrained models, from BERT-scale encoders to 8B-parameter language models, which require GPUs at inference time and are difficult to deploy on the monitoring nodes where logs are produced. We present LogCDM, a compact detector that scores each log session using three complementary signals: the next-event surprise of a small causal Transformer, the distance between a graph-attention embedding of the session's own transition graph and that of a normal reference graph, and a transition-drift statistic that we show is exactly a frequency-weighted chi-squared divergence between transition kernels. The divergence view yields an asymptotic null distribution, a length-standardized score, and a per-edge decomposition that explains each alarm. A sigmoid gate fuses the three signals; under a shared-covariance Gaussian model, this gate is the Bayes posterior, and its learned weights indicate which signal each dataset relies on. With 8.1-8.8M parameters and no GPU required at inference, LogCDM reaches F1 scores of 0.984 on HDFS, 0.870 on chronologically split BGL, and 0.850 on Thunderbird, within 0.013-0.12 of an 8.1B-parameter LLM detector evaluated under the same protocol and above all compact baselines on BGL. It scores a session in 5.5-10.5 ms on a CPU. Ablations show that the structural signals add 0.025-0.030 F1 on BGL and Thunderbird but almost nothing on HDFS, consistent with the learned fusion weights.

摘要：

##### **Library of Hands: Fix the Hands, Not the World Model**
2611.12678v1 by Hasaan Ahmad, Qizhen Ying, Yuxuan Sun, Tianjin Huang, Guangming Wang

A world model predicts how a scene changes after an action, and a planner uses it to search for actions that reach a goal. If the camera moves or the controller changes, the same command moves the robot differently: when its commands are turned by 90 degrees, a frozen planner with the LeWM world model that succeeds in 77.5% of episodes succeeds in only 7.8%. Current fixes retrain the model, need training data, or forget a set-up when it returns. We propose Library of Hands (LoH), a training-free method that keeps the world model frozen and corrects only the map from commands to applied actions, which we call the hand. LoH reads the hand through the frozen model, with no extra sensor: it finds the action that best explains the change between two camera images (a reading). It stores every hand it identifies, so a returning set-up is recognised rather than re-learnt. In closed-loop tests, LoH more than doubles success on three simulated tasks, from 28.6% to 62.3%, and loses only 0.6 percentage points when nothing changes. It also works, unchanged, with two other world models and on two of three models pretrained on real robot data, and its gain grows with the quality of its readings.

摘要：

##### **Interpretability Is Not Model-Specific**
2611.12675v1 by Marcus Armstrong, Navid Ayoobi, Pradham Mummaleti, Alexander Chulzhanov, Arjun Mukherjee

Interpretability tools are usually built for one model at a time: a refusal direction, a truthfulness probe, or a steering vector is found with labeled data inside the model it explains. We ask whether such tools can be carried to other models through a map between residual streams fit only on unlabeled prompts. We transfer a refusal direction, tested causally by addition and ablation, and two read-out tools, a correctness direction and a sentiment probe, across 110 pairs of models from five families and 1B to 72B parameters and between base and instruction-tuned models, with controls for specificity and five seeds. How much transfers depends on what the map was fit on. A map fit on ordinary questions recovers 54\% of the target's own refusal steering and 16\% to 77\% of its correctness gain; adding unlabeled prompts from the region where the tool operates raises these to 102\% and 88\% to 98\%, while control directions recover at most 1\%. We prove that a linear map behaves this way whenever two models correspond linearly on the data it covers, and a label-free coverage score derived from this result tracks recovery across more than 16,000 runs and explains earlier reports that maps between models fail across domains. Carried read-out tools outperform anything the target's unlabeled representations yield. Interpretability results, once found, can be reused across models at the cost of a few unlabeled prompts.

摘要：

##### **TrialDesignBench: Benchmarking Language Models for Phase-I Trial Design Against Clinical Knowledge**
2611.12640v1 by Yihan Hank Tang, Peng Yang, Jiayi Xin, Tianqi Shang, Xuyang Chen, Ying Yuan, Yi Lian, Qi Long

AI is used in much of the clinical trial pipeline. However, selecting the statistical design of a trial, a crucial step that determines how drug doses are tested on humans and evaluated, has seen almost no AI progress. LLMs appear to be a natural fit given their strengths in biomedical knowledge and statistical reasoning. Nevertheless, there is currently no appropriate way to evaluate such abilities because recent regulatory and methodological shifts, most notably FDA's Project Optimus, have redefined what constitutes a reasonable design and rendered many previous designs obsolete. To lay the groundwork for AI-assisted trial design, we introduce TrialDesignBench, a benchmark of 617 Phase-I/I-II oncology dose-finding trials, each paired with an expert-validated reference design that is consistent with the latest FDA regulatory initiatives. This reference standard is produced through a formalization of the expert design process. We evaluate five frontier LLMs, none of which matches the reference standard. Our ablation experiments trace this performance gap to two main causes, inference of latent, unrecorded design determinants from trial sources (supplying them raises class-wise balanced accuracy by 0.22) and structured decision-tree reasoning (+0.24). TrialDesignBench offers a reproducible, contemporary-standard objective for evaluating and enhancing AI-assisted trial design.

摘要：

##### **Clinical predictions using artificial intelligence in neurosurgery and its subspecialties: a systematic review and meta-analysis**
2611.12385v1 by Prajwal Ghimire, Aswin Chari, Jonathan Shapey, Keyoumars Ashkan

Artificial intelligence models for clinical prediction are proliferating across neurosurgery, but their aggregate discriminative performance, distribution along the care pathway and methodological quality are unclear. We systematically reviewed and meta-analysed these studies in neurosurgery and its subspecialities along with their risk of bias appraisal. We performed a PRISMA search for peer-reviewed original studies using AI to predict a clinical outcome in neurosurgical populations. Metrics were pooled using random effects modelling. Each study was classified as diagnostic, prognostic or forecasting and was assigned an evidence level. Clinical prediction task sub-stratification analysis was performed. Risk-of-bias was assessed using PROBAST. 1063 studies were included. Across 451 studies reporting AUC, pooled discrimination was 0.86, ranging from 0.84 (spine) to 0.87 (neuro-oncology), with extreme heterogeneity. Performance varied by clinical pathway stage, highest for diagnosis, grading and molecular tasks and lowest for post-operative tasks. Only 51 studies were using prospective data with external validation, whereas 619 rested on internal validation alone or no validation. 89 percent studies were at high risk-of-bias, driven by the analysis domain. AI models report high performance across all the neurosurgical subspecialities, concordant across AUC, accuracy, sensitivity and specificity and robust to risk-of-bias stratification. Performance is highest for imaging-anchored diagnostic tasks and lowest at decision-facing post-operative stage. However, the literature is dominated by optimism-prone, internally-validated and poorly calibrated models with extreme heterogeneity. External validation, calibration analysis and reporting guidelines adherence remain prerequisites for clinical adoption.

摘要：

##### **Code-Grounded Agentic Explanations for Static Analysis Vulnerability Findings**
2611.13041v1 by Oshando Johnson, Ranjith Krishnamurthy, Rohith Kumar Shanmuganathan, Goran Piskachev, Eric Bodden

Static application security testing (SAST) tools surface vulnerabilities early and their verdicts are rule-based and auditable. Yet developers routinely dismiss findings they cannot interpret. Large language models can supply the missing explanation, but designs that also hand the model the detection give up the auditability that makes static analysis defensible. We keep detection with the analyser and give the model only the explaining. A preliminary user study with 20 participants and a workshop with 19 practitioners yielded five requirements. Guided by these we redesigned SAFE, an open-source IntelliJ IDEA plugin that explains findings from any analyser reporting in the Static Analysis Results Interchange Format. It tiers explanations by demand, ties them to the reported data flow, and replaces the single-prompt backend with an agent that must consult the repository and the rule that fired before answering. A within-subjects crossover experiment with 24 participants compared the redesign against its predecessor. Perceived usability rose from 77.3 to 85.0 on the System Usability Scale. Actionability gained most among the explanation-quality items, the aspect both earlier studies flagged as weakest, and the odds of a correct remediation were 2.6 times higher. Triage accuracy and time per finding were indistinguishable, which we read as uninformative rather than as equivalence. Trust was the one measure that did not move, and the closing interviews indicate that grounding made findings easier to understand rather than easier to believe. Participants also drew a boundary no requirement anticipated, wanting the tool to find and explain while they judge and apply.

#### Abstracts
##### **Ambient LLMs: Data-Efficient Supervised Fine-tuning via Temperature Matching**
2611.23229v1 by Giannis Daras, Marios Papachristou, Adam Klivans, Antonio Torralba, Constantinos Daskalakis

Collecting large-scale, diverse, and high-quality post-training datasets for autoregressive language models is challenging. Consequently, Supervised Fine-Tuning (SFT) is typically applied to datasets significantly smaller than those used for pre-training, which leads to overfitting and the catastrophic forgetting of the tails of the data distribution. To prevent this collapse, we introduce Ambient SFT, a principled framework for leveraging imperfect predictions to augment the fine-tuning set. In our method, a "frozen" version of the pre-trained model serves as a noisy teacher that the fine-tuned model must match at a coarse level. In particular, we require that the high-temperature versions of the conditional distributions produced by the post-training model should match the high-temperature versions of the pre-training ones. The temperature hyperparameter controls the level of trust in the pre-trained model, with the limit $T\to \infty$ reverting back to regular SFT and $T=1$ to knowledge distillation. We demonstrate that our approach outperforms standard SFT across domain adaptation, instruction-tuning, and reasoning, leading for up to $21\%$ average relative improvement to standard SFT.

摘要：

##### **WoRD: Diffusion-Based Posterior Inference for Online Goal Recognition**
2611.23192v1 by Almog Anschel, Sarah Keren

Goal recognition is the task of inferring an agent's intended goal from its observed behavior. Most existing work focuses on high-level symbolic recognition, abstracting away the continuous motion through which agents reach their goal. Methods that do reason over continuous trajectories rely on an explicit dynamics model that is hard to acquire and rarely transfers across settings. Neither approach explicitly learns how agents move toward each candidate goal, limiting accuracy on ambiguous trajectory prefixes where such knowledge is most needed.
  We present WoRD (Waypoint Recognition with Diffusion), which uses a learned trajectory prior to infer the agent's goal from partial observations. At each step, WoRD runs one warm-started denoising chain per candidate goal, steers each chain toward the observed trajectory through a closed-form log-likelihood gradient, and reweights the resulting samples by Bayes' rule into a calibrated posterior over goals. We evaluate WoRD on simulated robot settings, pedestrian trajectories, and vehicle trajectories using four metrics targeting distinct failure modes. Across all three settings, the same inference procedure preserves calibrated multimodal beliefs through ambiguous prefixes where filter- and classifier-based observers collapse to a single mode or grow overconfident.

摘要：

##### **Retrieval-Augmented Correction for Generalist Medical Image Segmentation**
2611.23189v1 by Vincenzo Marcianò, Xiaoming Zhang, Michela Antonelli, Sebastien Ourselin, Maria A Zuluaga

Automated segmentation is routinely used as the first step in quantitative image-based clinical workflows, where predicted masks are subsequently reviewed and corrected by human operators. Automated correction of the residual structural errors is currently addressed one anatomy at a time, so the number of trained correctors scales with the number of segmentation targets. We present GRACE, a retrieval-augmented refiner that edits an input mask under context drawn from a shared knowledge base of verified prior cases. The refiner is trained once on a multi-organ corpus spanning compact organs, cavities, branching vessels, and tumour lesions. In the held-out dataset-transfer evaluation, three held-out datasets query a fixed knowledge base built from training data only. A separate leave-one-anatomy-out experiment tests parameter-free index extension: verified cases of an unseen solid organ enter the knowledge base, and the refiner stays frozen. A light shape-aware projection head on a frozen multimodal encoder maps embedding similarity to the shape agreement of the masks, so the knowledge base returns cases that the refiner can use as a shape prior. We evaluate GRACE on 18 public datasets spanning CT, MRI and ultrasound, using nine refinement configurations and seven upstream segmentors that span weak, mid, and strong operating regimes. We report each change with the DSC of its input mask. On the weak tier, the input DSC is 0.386 and GRACE adds up to +0.35 DSC. On a strong segmentor, the input DSC is 0.932 and the mean change is small. Transfer to an imaging modality that is absent from training and from the index is partial and depends on the anatomy, and remains an open research direction. Our results suggest retrieval-conditioned refinement is a practical alternative to anatomy-specific correction models. Code: https://github.com/robustml-eurecom/grace

摘要：

##### **The Illusion of Grounding: Do Vision-Language Models Weigh Retrieved Text and Images Like an Ideal Observer?**
2611.23127v1 by Basavaraj R M, Harshal Chandel

Retrieval-augmented vision-language models (VLMs) answer from two sources of evidence that can each fail: the retrieved text can be wrong or noisy, and the image can be degraded or contradicted. We introduce GIB-200K, 200,000 image-question items built from COCO and Visual Genome, each expanded into controlled stress conditions: hard-negative retrieval noise (1-20 documents), visual ambiguity (Gaussian and motion blur, low-resource crops) and cross-modal conflict with counterfactual flip pairs. Instead of only measuring degradation, we ask how models combine the two modalities. From ideal Bayesian cue combination we derive a testable prediction that yields formal definitions of Knowledge Inertia (text over-weighted relative to the model's own image evidence) and Sensory Capture (the reverse), and we estimate the weights with a measurement-error-corrected regression whose recovery of known weights we verify. A counterfactual flip decomposition separates modality bias from concept priors, and a Grounding Illusion Score measures how much clean accuracy is not grounded in the image. We evaluate 4 open models on one frozen, seeded evaluation split of 2,000 GIB-200K items. All models are accurate on clean inputs (92.5%-94.2%) but adverse accuracy falls to 41.3%-59.9%. The two LLaVA models over-weight the text (Knowledge Inertia, w_T/w_I = 1.229 and 1.322), whereas InternVL3 favours the image (w_T/w_I = 0.680); between 34.8% and 71.4% of the clean answers that are correct are abandoned when the document lies. A training-free consistency check that compares the answer with and without retrieval trades coverage for accuracy under misleading documents, while falling back to the image fails when the image is heavily degraded. All per-instance outputs, code, and an audit that recomputes every reported open-model number are released.

摘要：

##### **Large-scale Neuron Community Discovery Reveals Neuron-Sparse Behaviors in Language Models**
2611.23112v1 by Igor Shilov, Thomas Fel, Tal Haklay, Lucius Bushnaq, Owen Lewis

What reusable units of computation can we discover within a language model's neurons? Prior work has shown that individual forward passes can often be sparsified post-hoc to small groups of neurons, but it has been unclear whether these groups form coherent computational entities reused across inputs. We introduce neuron community discovery, an unsupervised method for finding neuron groups that recur across prompts and are causally necessary and sufficient, within the layers they span, for common computations. The method combines gradient-based attribution, a graph connecting neurons that are repeatedly attributed together, and community detection. Applied to Llama-3.1-8B using ten million prompts, it discovers thousands of candidate communities. We evaluate them on held-out prompts through three causal tests: community necessity, sufficiency within a layer subset, and layer necessity. These tests identify groups whose removal disrupts predictions and whose retention preserves predictions despite ablating the remaining MLP neurons in the tested layers. Discovered communities support functions like grammatical agreement, token-probability calibration, fixed phrases, in-context copying, addition, and multiplication. With communities capped at 128 neurons across all layers, 10.3% of held-out prompts have at least one layer's contribution explained on a neuron-sparse basis. Coverage remains 8.7% when requiring at least four layers and 3.3% when requiring at least twelve. These results show that small groups of existing neurons can provide reusable computational components across prompts, and that such groups can be discovered at scale.

摘要：

##### **Natural Language Programming: Rethinking Language, Memory, and Cognition in the Era of Generative AI**
2611.23106v1 by Giovanni Adorni, Daniele Grosso

Generative Artificial Intelligence is transforming the role of natural language in computational systems. In classical Natural Language Processing (NLP), language was treated as an object of computation: something to analyse, classify, translate, or generate. Large Language Models (LLMs) challenge this perspective, enabling natural language to function increasingly as an executable cognitive medium through which humans structure reasoning, orchestrate problem solving, and regulate hybrid human/AI workflows.
  This paper introduces Natural Language Programming (NLPg) as a theoretical and epistemological extension of traditional NLP. Rather than focusing on language understanding alone, NLPg conceptualises prompts as dynamic cognitive artefacts that externalise goals, constraints, assumptions, validation criteria, and intermediate reasoning states. Prompting is thus not merely a technical interaction technique, but a form of dialogic cognitive programming operating within distributed human/AI memory architectures.
  Building on theories of distributed cognition, extended mind, metacognition, and dialogic learning, the paper proposes the notion of Dialogic Working Memory, describing how prompts and conversational states function as temporary external memory structures supporting reasoning continuity and reflective regulation. We further map existing memory architectures (BTMA, SKGE, RMR) onto this framework, illustrating how episodic, semantic, and metacognitive layers support long-horizon conversational coherence.
  Finally, implications for computational linguistics and AI/mediated knowledge systems are discussed, suggesting a post symbolic and post interface paradigm in which natural language operates simultaneously as communication medium, executable specification, cognitive scaffold, and distributed memory architecture.

摘要：

##### **Learning to Undo: Transfer Reinforcement Learning under State Space Transformations**
2611.23081v1 by Mridul Mahajan, Aldo Pacchiano, Xuezhou Zhang

Transfer learning in reinforcement learning (RL) has shown strong empirical success. In this work, we take a more principled perspective by studying when and how transferring knowledge between MDPs (a source and a target) can be provably beneficial. Specifically, we consider the case where there exists an undo map such that applying this map to the target's state space recovers the source exactly. We propose an algorithm that learns this map by matching state feature statistics gathered from both MDPs, and then uses it to transfer the source policy to the target. We theoretically justify the algorithm by analyzing the setting when the undo map is linear and the source is linearly-$Q^\star$ realizable, where our approach has strictly better sample complexity than tabula rasa RL in the target MDP. Empirically, we demonstrate that these benefits extend beyond this regime: on challenging continuous control tasks and Atari games, our method achieves significantly better sample efficiency. Overall, our results highlight how shared structure between tasks can be leveraged for efficient transfer of policies across environments.

摘要：

##### **Colombo: A Frontier-to-Frontier Autoresearch System for Oracle-Based Optimization**
2611.23080v1 by Haihan Zhang, Wendao Wu, Chenheng Zhang, Chunyuan Zheng, Yanyi Li, Cong Fang, Haoxuan Li, Zhouchen Lin

Science advances from frontier to frontier as discoveries reshape what is known and create the basis for further discovery. We present Colombo, a self-organizing AI researcher that drives this renewal, first instantiated in oracle-based optimization. Here, the central question is how efficiently a problem class can be solved through a prescribed information interface and what costs no admissible algorithm can avoid. The gap between achievable guarantees and unavoidable limits defines the frontier recorded in its Complexity Atlas, a living map of frontier settings and the current state of knowledge. Self-Organization turns unresolved gaps into adaptive research programmes, coordinating conjecture formation, mathematical investigation, and replanning as evidence accumulates. Adversarial Co-Ascent develops algorithms and hard families through reciprocal challenge, exposing mechanisms of information use and concealment that guide stronger methods and proofs of unavoidable costs. Independent review and Lean-based formal certification check mathematical claims, while the Long-Term Memory carries supported findings and research experience across settings. To date, Colombo has established optimal or near-optimal rates for more than fifty settings in the Atlas. Colombo thus takes a concrete step toward Autopoietic Science: a scientific process whose validated advances regenerate the conditions of its own progress, continually reshaping how inquiry is organized and what it can discover.

摘要：

##### **ReTouchGS: Ultralight Render-free Aesthetic Color Enhancement and Transfer for 3D Gaussian Splatting**
2611.23054v1 by Chuanzhi Xu, Boyu Wei, Chengkun Yue, Xuanhua Yin, Yiyang Li, Haodong Chen, Qiang Qu, Weidong Cai

3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) enables photorealistic scene reconstruction. Existing 3DGS color transfer and interactive editing methods are guided by reference images and user-specified color adjustments. However, in many applications, users simply want aesthetically guided color enhancement that automatically makes the scene more visually appealing while maintaining consistent colors and tones across viewpoints. We present ReTouchGS, to our knowledge the first framework for reference-free aesthetic color enhancement of pretrained 3DGS models. It predicts a scene-level color lookup table (LUT) from the model and its associated camera parameters. ReTouchGS also supports reference-guided color transfer by fitting a scene-level LUT using one edited view of the same scene and its corresponding original render. A shared blend-aware color refinement network with only 44.7K parameters refines an analytic color update to realize either color mapping without rendering. It preserves geometry and delivers standard 3DGS assets in seconds. We also design the ReTouch3D benchmark to support the evaluation of aesthetic color enhancement and reference-guided color transfer across viewpoints. Experiments demonstrate high color accuracy and strong generalization to unseen scenes in both aesthetic color enhancement and reference-guided color transfer.

摘要：

##### **Can Decision Models Replace LLMs in Real-World Contact Center Classification and Judging Tasks?**
2611.22949v1 by Md Tahmid Rahman Laskar, Xue-Yong Fu, Shashi Bhushan TN

Contact center systems often need to make bounded decisions, such as identifying a caller's intent, retrieving the knowledge-base article that answers a query, assessing agent compliance, or judging the output of AI models used in production. Decision models return a typed answer and its probability directly, making them a promising cost-effective alternative to generative Large Language Models (LLMs) in such bounded conversational tasks. However, their effectiveness in real-world contact center applications remains unclear. In this paper, we compare nine decision models against thirteen generative LLMs in zero-shot settings across eight contact center use cases: six classification tasks and two judging tasks. Our experiments demonstrate that many decision models can match the performance of frontier LLMs on contact center classification, and API-based closed decision models achieve this at the lowest per-request cost among the API models we evaluate. We further observe that their confident predictions are more accurate, enabling selective routing in real-world production scenarios. Across classification tasks, the main weakness of most decision models is abstention: they rarely choose the no-answer option, which accounts for most of their gap to the strongest LLMs (although Gemini-Decision-1 is the notable exception in this regard). We also find that the judging quality of decision models drops sharply for smaller models, and they are also more prone to position bias. In short, our findings provide practical guidance for selecting decision models based on abstention behavior, confidence, and deployment cost, rather than overall accuracy alone.

摘要：

##### **Better Rankers, Worse Retrievers: The Hidden Cost of RL Post-Training in Generative Recommendation**
2611.22838v1 by Yuheng Chen, Fan Zhang, Yixuan Xu, Chao Yang, Yang Xiang, Can Yang

Generative recommenders decode the semantic ID of a user's next item with a language model, and post-training them with reinforcement learning (RL) after supervised fine-tuning (SFT) has become standard practice. The gains are mostly reported on the first ten results, yet in deployment such models hand hundreds of candidates to a downstream ranker or reward model. We show that RL post-training has a hidden cost: it improves accuracy at the top of the list (hit@K for small K) but, on all five datasets we study, lowers the recall of the candidate list (hit@K for K in the hundreds). The model becomes a better ranker and a worse retriever. Controlled experiments trace the loss to the negative advantages that RL assigns to the candidates it samples, the rollout. The root cause is that only one next item is logged per user, so RL treats every other candidate as wrong. Yet these candidates are the model's most plausible alternatives, and often the next items of other users with similar histories. Penalising them pushes down exactly what similar users choose next, and these items drop out of those users' candidate lists. This diagnosis points directly to a remedy: out-of-rollout anchoring, a single additional loss term that preserves the SFT model's ordering of the items outside the rollout. It keeps the gain at the top and restores recall to the SFT level on all five datasets, and it is effective under other RL configurations as well. To our knowledge, this is the first systematic study of this recall loss: we measure it, identify its cause, and show that, once the cause is known, a simple remedy suffices.

摘要：

##### **A tiny object detection dataset for high-altitude open-water search and rescue**
2611.22825v1 by Hui Xu, Chenfei Hou, Henglai Wei, Hao Wang, Yidong Jin, Xiulin Niu, Haoyang Zhang, Hui Zhang, Tianshi Xu

Large aerial platforms often operate above 1.5 km to improve the efficiency of open water search and rescue (OW-SAR). However, long imaging distances, wide fields of view, and limited sensor resolution reduce objects to a few pixels, increasing their susceptibility to environmental interference and making detection harder than for conventional small objects. To our knowledge, existing datasets focus on low-altitude scenarios and do not support automatic detection at 1.5-2.0 km. We therefore present TinyWOD, a tiny object detection dataset for high-altitude OW-SAR using lifebuoys as detection targets. It comprises 11,359 manually annotated images and 19,505 annotated instances spanning diverse environments, lighting conditions, water colors, and surface ripples. Compared with existing datasets, TinyWOD contains much smaller objects and a substantially higher proportion of tiny instances; its average equivalent side length (ESL) is 12.7 pixels, with 76.96% of in stances having ESLs below 15 pixels, reflecting actual high-altitude water-rescue imaging conditions. We define APtiny, a tiny object detection metric, and benchmark mainstream and state-of-the-art networks using this and conventional metrics. All evaluated networks effectively detect tiny waterborne objects, demonstrating TinyWOD's reusability and utility. We expect this dataset to support research on tiny object detection in high-altitude OW-SAR.

摘要：

##### **Pluggable Subband-Decoupled Fusion and Unlabeled Capture Prototype Calibration: Bridging High-SNR Null-Subcarrier Regrowth and Low-SNR Coherent Preambles for Cross-Domain IoT RF Fingerprinting**
2611.22812v1 by Fengchong Yao, Jianbing Li, Qing Liu, Kefeng Song, Haitao Li, Song Wang, Kunpeng Wang

Zero-overhead physical-layer authentication at edge wireless access points (APs) is vital for securing resource-constrained Internet of Things (IoT) transceivers against credential cloning, yet cross-domain IEEE 802.11a/g radio frequency fingerprint identification (RFFI) faces an acute signal-to-noise ratio (SNR) crossover. Power-amplifier spectral regrowth on unpopulated null subcarriers resists indoor multipath at high SNR but decays quadratically below the thermal noise floor, whereas populated active in-band preambles survive low SNR yet degrade under severe multipath, causing static fusion to lose up to 44.7 percentage points (pp). To our knowledge, we propose the first RFFI framework that dynamically modulates active in-band waveforms and unpopulated null-subcarrier regrowth via a blind physical SNR gate: Pluggable Gated Subband-Decoupled Fusion with Unlabeled Capture-Aware Prototype Calibration (GSDF-CAPC). Avoiding the 20-35 dB subband gradient imbalance of joint training, GSDF pairs any frozen pretrained host backbone with a 46.3K null-subcarrier plug-in encoder (NS-Branch), fusing scale-standardized logits and gate-warped spherical embeddings under a source-calibrated lag-64 thermal-SNR gate with exact low-SNR fallback. Over unlabeled target AP captures, forward-only unbalanced optimal-transport calibration (UOT-CAPC) aligns shifted prototypes under packet-arrival skew and recovers parameter-free balanced Sinkhorn (U-CAPC) at equipartition. Through experiments across multiple model families and datasets, together with theoretical analysis, we identify three main findings. First, unlike the relatively gradual propagation observed in traditional offline distillation, backdoors rarely emerge in OPD at low poisoning ratios. Instead, it is driven by the continuous accumulation of anomalous probability mass assigned by the teacher. Finally, based on these findings, we propose a novel trigger design that enables visual backdoor propagation with zero explicit trigger injection. ##### **Symbolic Abstraction Refinement**
2611.22404v1 by Daniel Bryce, Robert P. Goldman, David J. Musliner, Jeremy Gottlieb, John Skovbekk

Verification normally works over symbolic states, each pairing a discrete location with a region of clock values, and its difficulty is that the best behavior generally varies within such a region. The PRISM verifier's most capable PTA method responds by splitting regions until each is uniform, rebuilding the transitions that reach them and propagating the split backward through the state graph of a stochastic game, a cascade of graph reconstruction that partitions regions the property never depends on. We present SYMAR, which instead makes the value function symbolic: each symbolic state carries a function from sub-regions of its clock region to probability bounds, so refinement is the appearance of new sub-regions in that function, and previously generated states are never split nor their incoming transitions reconstructed. Both sources of PTA nondeterminism, the timing and the choice of actions, are captured implicitly by the preimage computation that produces those sub-regions. Heuristic forward search then bounds how much refinement is done at all, expanding an And-Or graph from the initial state along the most promising behaviors so that effort is confined to the property-relevant fragment, guided by bounds and preferred actions from a single unrefined run of the existing method; a partial-refinement backup keeps the otherwise combinatorial sub-region count lean, and every stored interval provably contains the true probability. On the PRISM PTA benchmarks, whose subproblems recur in autonomous systems (distributed agreement under contention, leader election, and self-configuration without central coordination), SYMAR is up to 108x faster, solves an instance on which the existing method exhausts the time limit, and is markedly less sensitive to the deadline constant, resolving one instance in a 37-node search graph, unchanged as the deadline triples, where the existing method needs 86,380 abstract states and 73 refinement rounds.

##### **Expertise capture in workplace AI**

AI assistants are becoming part of how knowledge workers think and perform, accumulating the prompts, exemplars, memory and interaction histories that encode a worker's professional judgment. We name the resulting process expertise capture, the absorption of worker-applied knowledge and judgment into AI resources under organizational control. We develop a framework of contribution forms, technical residences and transformation families, and show that how far a contribution can be attributed, separated and revoked depends on where it resides and how it has been transformed, properties fixed by system design long before any dispute arises. System designs were mapped onto shared architectural modules, identifying nine recurrent responsibilities of the home local server. Generative models support perceptual interpretation, intent understanding, and candidate planning, while deterministic validation, knowledge-graph constraints, and authorization leases confine candidate actions to an inspectable space. Home control, emotional companionship, and intelligent guarding can be compared within a common framework: they share state and memory but differ in latency, false positive and negative rates, proactivity, and permissions. A multi-output tree ensemble predicts several binary labels through decision paths that it largely shares across those labels, yet the attribution methods commonly used to explain such models treat each output in isolation. We propose multi-sink Decision Predicate Graphs (ms-DPGs), which extend decision predicate graphs with one sink per label: each leaf contributes to the sink of every label its in-bag positive count for that label. On this representation we define Shared Predicate Flow (SPF), the share of a label's decision mass that passes through conditions active for both labels of a pair, and a contracted view of the graph in which label-specific lifts indicate whether a shared condition raises the probability of both labels or of one at the expense of the other. Controlled experiments show that SPF captures shared decision conditions rather than mere feature overlap. On nine multi-label benchmarks, native multi-output forests share substantially more decision structure than Binary Relevance on eight, including five of the six on which the two are at predictive parity, even at equal hyperparameters, whereas per-label TreeSHAP rankings register the change of architecture without showing whether more is shared. Because a native forest routes every label through the same splits, SPF describes the fitted model rather than the data, and on eight of the nine benchmarks it rises when the feature-label signal is removed; a high SPF is therefore not evidence of shared predictive information. ms-DPG offers a global, predicate-level account of how a multi-output ensemble reuses decision conditions across its labels.
